The Thiele Machine
A Computational Model with Explicit Structural Cost
        Structure, records, observation, and cost




                     Devon Thiele
                 September 2026 – v3.3.0
            Before the book: the short version, and how to check it

I didn’t invent a machine. I found one.

That is how I see this work. I think there is structure here that the usual picture of
computation leaves out. The conviction brought me to the project; the definitions and
proofs are how I put it to the test.

What the Thiele Machine is. It is an abstract model of computation. It is a machine
the way Turing’s machine is a machine: mathematics you can reason about, not a
device. It is not a CPU. It is not an instruction set, a VM, a graph, or category theory.
Those are things I built with it, or things it can carry.

The model is small. A state. A step rule. A ledger that only climbs. A rule for which
steps have to pay. And a window: what an observer is allowed to see of the state. Like
a Turing machine, it is a precise way to say what a computation is. Unlike a Turing
machine, it does not compute anything new. It adds what a computation establishes,
and what establishing it cost.

What this book is. It is an argument, made with that model. The argument is about
what an account of computation should preserve.

I built this because the answer at the end of a computation did not tell me everything
I wanted to know. What structure did the machine use? What did it establish? What
did it pay to establish it? I wanted those questions in the mathematics, with definitions
somebody else could check.

The usual account describes a computation by what it can read and write: memory,
registers, where the program is. I think that account is a shadow. There is another axis.
It holds what the computation established, and what that cost. Here is the argument,
one step at a time, with where each step stands.

   1. The shadow loses something real. Two runs can look identical through the usual
      window and still differ in what they established and what they paid. Nothing
      reading only the window gets the difference back. Proved, for each window I
      name.
   2. Establishing is not free. If the step that turns uncertified into certified has to pay,
      every run that starts uncertified and ends certified has paid. Proved, for any
      system that follows that rule, whatever its states and instructions are. And the
      rule itself isn’t arbitrary. On a machine with finite memory, a certificate that can
      never be revoked can only be switched on by a step that merges two states, and
      merging is what Landauer’s principle charges for whenever the machine could
The Thiele Machine                                          Devon Thiele • September 2026


      be in either state. Proved, with Landauer’s principle as the named premise. The
      heat comes from that uncertainty: a machine already known to be in one state
      loses nothing, and that is proved too. Learning isn’t what costs. A measurement
      that forgets nothing can be priced at zero by an account that prices every merge,
      and an observer still learns from it. That is proved too. What costs is committing
      to what you learned, in a record the machine can’t take back. A small finite
      piece of the VM is such a machine: programs built from CERTIFY 0, JUMP a 2
      and CHECKPOINT. The VM runs them paying exactly what their merges cost,
      and the JUMP charge of two is the program’s own choice of µ-delta. In the
      full VM, every certification flip is a priced merge, while JUMP 0 0 is a zero-cost
      merge. Thus the VM does not price every merge; the theorem does not classify
      all non-certifying merges.
   3. Leaning on a fact takes a paid history. A computation is entitled to lean on a
      structural fact when it holds the evidence for it and a history that earned it.
      Defined precisely; the cost floor is proved for shortcuts that hand over their
      receipts.
   4. So the axis belongs in the step. An account of computation should carry what was
      established and what it cost, as part of how it steps. Not as something a program
      bolts on afterward. Proposed. This is the conviction the proofs are there to test.
      The proved part is exact. If the account is going to charge certification exactly,
      never less and never more, the charge can’t be computed from the window. A
      step that certifies and a step that doesn’t can look the same through it, so any
      charge read off the window either misses the one or bills the other. The exact
      price needs the reading inside what the step carries. Proved, for any machine
      with such a pair, and the VM has one for both windows I name. On a machine
      with finite memory, an account that prices merging steps the way Landauer’s
      principle does, one unit per halving, already charges every step that writes a
      permanent record, at least the logarithm of how many states it squeezes together.
      The same bound holds in bits of Shannon entropy. Whether every account has
      to price merges is physics, not a theorem.
   5. Which events every account has to price. Certification is the one I pinned down.
      For finite machines the answer is this: the events an account based on merge
      pricing must charge are the steps that merge states, every step that writes a
      record nothing takes back is one of them, and a flip escapes the price only when
      the same step can take the reading back somewhere. Proved, with Landauer’s
      principle as the named premise. The stronger guess, that the priced events are
      exactly the permanent records, is false, and a three-state counterexample shows
      it. No theorem selects another universal class of priced events. The pointer-
      observable conjecture is my candidate answer.

Certification is one point I could pin down. It gave me a law I could state, a VM I
could build and run, and proofs I could put in front of someone who did not share my


                                           2
The Thiele Machine                                                  Devon Thiele • September 2026


intuition. It is one witness in a much larger argument about structure and observation.
It is not the argument. The unproved reach is labeled as conjecture.

How the book is laid out. Part I is the argument. Part II is one build of the model:
a 51-opcode VM, the hardware it runs on, a category-theory layer for structure, and
a check against the CHSH bound. The build is there so the argument has something
you can run and try to break. It is a witness. It is not the subject. Part III is how to
check all of it. Part IV states the boundary of the proved results. The vocabulary I had
to learn is at the back, for anyone who wants it.

The shadow is real for the projections I proved it for. Drop the relevant distinction
and no decoder gets it back from what remains. A different encoding can keep it.
That is part of the question: which distinctions deserve to survive an account of a
computation?

I think there is something foundational here. I will be straight about the difference
between that conviction and a theorem. The results below have statements, premises,
and proof terms. The larger claim is a conjecture, not a theorem. I built the machine
so that work could be done in public, with something firmer than my confidence to
argue over.

                                            Abstract

          The Thiele Machine is an abstract model of computation, and this book is an
      argument made with it. The argument is about what a computation keeps and
      what keeping it costs. The usual account of a computation, what it reads and
      writes, is a shadow of it; the model adds the axis that account leaves out. It carries
      structure as part of its state, a ledger that only climbs, a rule for which events
      have to pay, and a window saying what an observer sees. The first result is about
      that window. Two runs can look identical through it and still differ in what they
      established and what they paid. No function of the named windows gets the
      difference back, and for the claim µ = 1 on a named colliding pair, no checker that
      reads only the window can be both sound and complete. That is a theorem about
      what the shadow loses. It is not true by definition. Certification is the one event I
      pinned down all the way. The rule is called A2: any step that flips a designated
      yes/no reading from false to true costs at least one. A2 is a rule I chose, and the
      cost theorem built on it sits close to that choice: once the flip is priced, every run
      that flips has paid. What A2 adds is where it lives. It is part of the step rule. It is
      not a program running on top of one. And for an exact price it has to be: when a
      step that certifies and a step that doesn’t look the same through the window, no
      charge computed from the window prices certification exactly, and the VM has
      such a pair for both windows I name. The cost theorem built on A2 doesn’t care
      what the states or instructions are. Any system that follows A2 is covered.
          Three results carry the weight of the structural question. Every deterministic
      machine can carry the record axis: the machine plus a latch on an event it reaches,
      charging one unit when the latch sets, is an honest extension of it (latch_core_
      honest). On the VM the record can’t be read back off the shadow: no function
      of the named windows returns certification or µ (cert_not_function_of_f


                                                3
The Thiele Machine                                                Devon Thiele • September 2026


      orget, mu_not_function_of_bare_observable). And on finite hardware
      a step that writes a permanent record merges two states (permanent_flip_i
      s_not_injective), so an account that prices merges already prices the write.
      That a permanent record, driven by the computation, is a latch on one event (rec
      ord_axis_is_latch_holds) is close to definitional; it follows from those two
      conditions in a few lines. The pointer question asks which event gets latched; no
      theorem here selects one uniquely.
           The formal results go further than that one floor. They pin down exact unit
      pricing relative to how many certification flips happen, show the ledger is unique
      once the schedule is fixed, exhibit states that a specified window can’t tell apart,
      give a universal property for folding traces, and check some specialized algebraic
      certificates. The executable VM and the hardware bridges are one build of the
      model. They realize parts of this, each under its stated conditions. They are wit-
      nesses to the argument. They are not its subject. Paying for an event is accounting.
      Truth needs a checker whose soundness is proved.
           Two questions lie outside the proved core: what the ledger means physically,
      and which events every account of computation has to price. For the full instruc-
      tion set, the F1 physical premise pair can’t coexist with free jumps, and F1_phys
      ical_premises_incompatible proves it. A narrower pair does hold together.
      On a machine with finite memory and a certificate that can never be revoked, the
      step that certifies has to merge two states, so pricing merges, as Landauer’s princi-
      ple does in the worst case over what the machine might hold, yields A2 (a2_fro
      m_merging_price_and_permanence). Priced per halving, switching k states
      on beside m certified ones costs at least log2 ((m + k )/m). In Shannon entropy
      the same step removes at least that many bits from the uniform distribution on
      the states in play, and with Landauer’s principle as the premise that is at least
      k B T ln((m + k)/m) of heat. At positive temperature, any distribution that leaves
      every state in play possible forces positive heat under the same premise; a state
      known in advance forces none, and both are proved. A four-slot piece of the VM
      with a certification flag is such a finite machine. For programs built from CERTIFY
      0, JUMP a 2 and CHECKPOINT the VM runs it step for step, and its ledger rises
      by exactly what the merges cost; the JUMP charge of two is the program’s cho-
      sen µ-delta. In the full VM every certification flip is a priced merge, and at least
      one other merge, JUMP 0 0, is free. And on finite hardware, under the premise
      that merges are priced, the events every such account must price are exactly the
      merges: every permanent-record write is one, and a flip escapes the price only
      when the same step can take it back. The physical side requires an experiment.
      The observation results don’t forbid encodings that keep the information. The
      trace-fold property doesn’t make the VM’s state space initial.

Part I

The argument
This part is the argument, and the model it is made with. Where a VM instruction
shows up here, it is an example of a step. The build it comes from is Part II.

1 Let me be straight with you
One thing before anything else, because people get it backwards. I used language
models to write the code and the proofs in this project, and I trust language models
about as far as I can throw them, which is exactly why every formal declaration is
checked by a proof assistant that does not care who or what wrote it. Coq rejects a
wrong proof the same whether a tenured professor or a model hands it over, and it
accepted these, with none of my own axioms anywhere in them, re-checkable using
the reproduction procedure in Section 21.1. Being self-taught and using modern tools
did not make this looser. It is the reason I made a machine check the formal corpus
and built separate tests and audits for the prose and implementation boundaries, me
included. The long version of that is in Section 2, under how the work actually got
made; I am putting the short version up here so it lands before anything else does.

What this project is. It’s an argument about what an account of computation should
preserve, made with an abstract model built to carry it. Its central proposal is structural
entitlement. A computation is entitled to lean on a structural fact when it holds the
evidence for it and a history that earned it. The model, its laws, and the instructions
of any one build are different things, and I keep them apart. Generic CERTIFY pays
to set a flag. It checks nothing. A claim that something is actually true needs explicit
witnesses and a checker proved sound. Some results here are that kind. The generic
A2 theorem isn’t one of them. It’s an accounting law.

The proved core of the argument, in three steps.

   1. Anything that follows A2 pays something to get from uncertified to certified.
   2. A window that shows two states as the same, when they disagree on the ledger
      or on some other question, can’t answer that question on every state.
   3. A checker that only sees evidence where the named pair collides can’t be both
      sound and complete about the claim µ = 1 that separates them.

Those are statements about named laws and named windows. An ordinary machine
can keep a richer encoding, or enforce the same invariant through its own transition
table. What I’m proposing is about why the extra distinctions matter. It isn’t a theorem
that every Turing-equivalent system lacks them.


                                             9
The Thiele Machine                                            Devon Thiele • September 2026


Substrate vs scaffolding. Two layers run through the rest of this document. I kept
conflating them myself until the proofs forced the line. Confuse them and the theorems
quietly stop meaning what they say.

The substrate is the shape: a kind of state, and a rule for stepping from one state to
the next. Different theorems ask that shape for different extra pieces, and the pieces
aren’t the same. No Free Insight asks for a certification reading and the A2 rule on
it: flipping it from false to true costs at least one. Ledger initiality asks for something
else, a fixed starting value and a fixed schedule of increments. Each theorem holds for
any state type and step rule that carries the pieces it asks for, whatever instruction set
builds them.

The structural-axis undecidability theorem in Section 10 asks for different pieces again.
The base substrate brings a ledger that never goes down, a way to write its own
programs down as states, and Kleene’s recursion theorem for the program transfor-
mations it can express. The WithShortcutPredicate extension adds a shortcut
predicate, one program it accepts, one it rejects, and the rule that programs which
behave the same get the same answer. None of that needs A2. The candidate-indexed
nat_substrate family that supplies its restricted fixed points carries no certification
content at all, by construction. So “substrate-level” doesn’t mean one fixed interface
every theorem shares. It means each result is stated over exactly the extension it uses.
It isn’t folded into the 51-opcode VM.

The scaffolding is everything that holds the shape up so you can touch it. That’s the
51 opcodes, the Python reference kernel, the extracted OCaml runtime, the Bluespec
hardware, and the simulator harness. The opcode semantics live here too, along
with the conditional correspondence between the Kami model and vm_apply, the
synthesized gate count, and the translations further down that are tested or trusted to
the tools. That’s how this particular substrate gets concrete enough that you can boot
it, audit it, and run it on silicon. The 51-opcode VM fills in several of the extensions
at once. It’s an A2-respecting CertCostMachine, and it’s a WithShortcutPredic
ate instance, among others. A substrate-level result still only borrows the extension
its own theorem names. That’s the line to hold onto. The VM is one instance filling
several extensions at the same time. It isn’t one flat interface called “the substrate.”
Let that line blur and a theorem stated over one extension starts reading as if it carried
premises it never uses.

What “new model of computation” means here. It adds something to how a machine
runs: structure it keeps, and an account of what that structure cost. It doesn’t add
computable functions. When I say “classical shadow” I mean the particular window or
fragment I name. I don’t mean every way a conventional machine could store things.
Enforcing the law, storing it, and letting an observer see it are separate questions. It is
an abstract model the way the Turing machine is. It isn’t a new primitive the way the
Turing machine is. A Turing machine builds computation out of a tape, a head, and a


                                            10
The Thiele Machine                                                       Devon Thiele • September 2026


                              Scaffolding one realisation of the substrate

                     Kami RTL • Bluespec • Verilog netlist • FPGA bitstream




                                            Substrate
                        state type · step relation, extended per theorem
                     No Free Insight, µ-initiality: A2 extension (µ ledger, A2 cost rule)
          structural-axis undecidability: WithShortcutPredicate extension (no A2 needed)




                     51 opcodes • Python kernel • OCaml runner • parity tests



Figure 1: Substrate inside, scaffolding around it. The substrate is a kind of state
and a step rule, and each theorem asks it for its own extra pieces. No Free Insight
needs the certification floor. Ledger initiality needs a starting value and an increment
schedule. Structural-axis undecidability needs the separate WithShortcutPredica
te extension and no A2 at all. Each theorem holds for any system carrying the pieces
it uses, whatever the instruction set. The scaffolding is what makes this particular
substrate something you can run, check, and synthesize. Merging the two layers, or
merging the two extensions into one, is the most common way this document gets
misread.

table. The substrate takes any kind of state and any step rule and asks them to obey a
law. So it sits on top of a machine. It doesn’t stand beside it. A Turing machine whose
table carries the rule is one of its instances. What I am claiming is narrower and, to me,
more interesting. The law, the structural state it prices, and what an observer can and
cannot see of that state belong in the step. They are not something a program bolts on
afterward.

The renderer experience is the entry point, and Section 2 tells it in full. I started by
trying to build a 3D engine where category-theoretic objects were the runtime objects.
Modules. Morphisms. Composition. Not data structures simulating those things. The
actual things. The classical CPU saw none of that. It shuffled bytes that happened to
encode the structural state. Z3 saw formulas that happened to refer to the same shapes.
The structural facts the engine was computing with were in the architecture, not in
the data. That is the concrete shape I had to formalise. The substrate this document
describes is what came out: structure as state, not as an approximation written on top
of state. Compositional structure was just the first kind I met.

The formal results turn that into a precise version of the shadow. thiele_simulat
es_turing lifts a Turing machine into the model, inside its own file-local transition
system. degenerate_projection_theorem pins down exactly what the chosen
window forgets: two states have equal six-field shadows exactly when they satisfy the


                                                  11
The Thiele Machine                                             Devon Thiele • September 2026


named equality relation. Keeping two executions in step takes more than that. The
graph and the CSRs have to agree too. The VM’s separation witnesses show which
structural distinctions each named window loses.

The irrecoverability theorems are about vm_certified, csr_cert_addr, and µ,
each under its named window. Once more than one state sits behind a view, nothing
gets every original back from it. You can still pick defaults to fill the gaps. The defaults
don’t tell you what was really there. And an encoding that keeps the information,
with a decoder that reads it, is always allowed.

What is claimed unique, and what is not. When I say there’s only one possible
substrate, I’m squeezing three different claims into one breath. This document proves
one of them.

The first is about the law. Given the A2 rule, is there basically one honest way to price
certification? Section 7 answers a version of that for a fixed schedule. instructio
n_consistent_measure_equals_mu shows that any measure using the kernel’s
own per-instruction schedule and starting at zero has to equal µ on every reachable
state. It doesn’t show the schedule itself is forced. A different schedule prices things
differently, and that’s a different accounting, just as honest.

The second is about the core. Strip every A2-respecting system down to what the
theorems actually use. Do they all turn out to be the same object, under the right idea
of “the same”? That’s the strongest reading of “only one substrate.” In the form the
words suggest, it is false.

Asking it precisely takes three definitions: what a record-carrying machine is, when
one is adequate, and when two are the same. StructuralCore.v fixes them. A
record-carrying machine is a deterministic machine with starting states, a yes/no
record reading, a ledger carried in the state, and a halting observation. It is adequate
when its ledger never goes down, its record satisfies A2, some run from a starting
state is certified, and each two-counter halting problem has a starting state whose
observed halt agrees with it. That last clause is an existential halting correspondence,
not a two-way simulation. Two machines are the same (core_equiv) when a relation
covers their starting states in both directions, and related states agree on the record
and on the price of the next step, and step to related states. The relation need not be
a function, so extra state one machine keeps can be ignored. None of this is literal
sameness of opcodes, storage layout, implementation language, or clock cycles.

StructuralCoreCover.v gives a stronger form, for machines that run the VM
underneath. An explicit projection onto the VM commutes with every step, preserves
halting, and reaches every VM starting state. The record is permanent, and some
reachable step writes it. Sameness also watches the absolute ledger balance and
halting.

Section 3.7 builds a target (the Coq witness is certification_agreement_does

                                            12
The Thiele Machine                                          Devon Thiele • September 2026


_not_imply_descent) that keeps the VM’s whole instruction history, charges the
same schedule, and reads certification the way the VM does. It agrees with the VM on
every certification and every charge. It still tells the empty trace apart from JUMP 0
0, two traces the VM treats as the same, because it kept the history and the VM didn’t.
A2 and matching certification don’t make that extra history fall away. The relational
sameness above is what lets it fall away: history_core_equiv_thiele proves
that its autonomous counterpart, a machine that keeps every earlier VM state, is the
same as the Thiele core.

The conjecture, in either form, is that every adequate machine is the same as the
Thiele core (adequate_core_uniqueness, honest_vm_extension_uniquene
ss). Both forms are false (adequate_core_uniqueness_refuted, honest_vm_
extension_uniqueness_refuted), with no axioms of any kind. The counterex-
ample is a machine that bills CPU time. Take the Thiele core and add a step counter
to the state. Let the ledger be the VM’s ledger plus the counter. Every step costs one
unit more than the same step of the Thiele core. It is adequate (billed_core_ade
quate), because A2 is a floor and a surcharge only raises the price. It runs the VM
underneath, with dropping the counter as the projection (billed_core_honest_e
xtension). It still isn’t the same. A Thiele state with an empty program never moves
and pays nothing per step. Every step of the billed machine costs at least one.

   Plain reading. Adequacy puts a floor on the price of certifying. It never forbids
   charging for other things. A machine that charges for time is adequate, and it isn’t
   the same machine.

The billed machine isn’t a different structure, though. It is the same machine with a
different price list. So the claim worth keeping is uniqueness up to the price schedule:
every honest record-carrying machine that runs the VM underneath and records the
VM’s own certification is the Thiele core plus some price schedule that satisfies A2.
StructuralCoreSchedule.v states it for machines that run the VM underneath.
Charges are not compared, every machine in play carries a monotone ledger that
satisfies A2, and sameness is read through the machine’s own projection onto the VM.
It comes in two strengths.

When the record is the VM’s own certification reading, uniqueness holds (cert_
record_schedule_uniqueness_holds). Relate each state to the VM state its
projection assigns, and everything lines up. The billing machine is an example, not a
counterexample: it is the Thiele core with a price of one extra unit per step (billed
_core_equiv_mod_schedule). So is the Thiele core with any bill that depends on
the current state, per step, per instruction, or per millisecond (surcharged_core_e
quiv_mod_schedule).

When the record only has to be some reading of the computation, uniqueness fails (t
ied_record_schedule_uniqueness_refuted). Take the Thiele core and let its


                                          13
The Thiele Machine                                           Devon Thiele • September 2026


record be “the meter has passed one.” That reading never switches off, a paid step
switches it on, and A2 holds for it. At a state with a positive meter and no certificate,
the two record lights disagree.

   Plain reading. One structure, two free choices. The price list is one. Which perma-
   nent reading counts as the record is the other.

The second choice is the pointer-observable question of Section 26: what makes cer-
tification the event to record. Both strengths take the VM’s structure as given, since
every machine they admit runs the VM.

StructuralCoreAnyBase.v drops the VM. A base is any deterministic machine
without a record: a Turing machine, a RAM, a reversible machine, the VM itself. A
machine extends a base when it runs the base step for step through a map down to it,
carrying whatever hidden state it likes. Give it a record that never switches off, that
some paid step writes, that satisfies A2, and whose next value depends only on the
base state and on itself. Then the base state and the record move exactly as a latch: the
record switches on the first time the base passes a state where some event h holds, and
it never switches off (record_axis_is_latch_holds). The latch form constrains
nothing else the machine carries; the rest can show up in its ledger. Two such records
move as two latches, each of whose events may read the other record (record_pair
_is_two_latches_holds).

Both conditions on the record do work. Over a counter base, a record that flips at
every step is not a latch of any event (toggle_not_latch). A record switched on by
a hidden clock never switches off, and it still isn’t driven by the computation (clock
_record_not_driven). Every base carries the axis: for any event the base reaches,
the latch of that event, priced one unit per write, is an honest extension (latch_c
ore_honest). So a RAM with a record driven by its computation is a RAM plus a
latch (record_axis_is_latch_on_ram_holds, for the Cook–Reckhow RAM). A
reversible machine with unbounded memory can carry one and stay reversible, by
keeping every earlier record value (history_latch_injective, history_latc
h_honest). With finite memory it can’t: a step that writes a permanent record there
is not injective (finite_reversible_cannot_write, from Section 9.1). Bases
are compared by weak_base_equiv: related states agree on an observation and on
halting, and each base matches the other’s steps up to stuttering. It is an equivalence
relation, and it preserves the latch form, which holds on the Turing machine, the VM,
the Cook–Reckhow RAM and L (record_axis_is_latch_on_tm_holds, reco
rd_axis_is_latch_on_vm_holds, record_axis_is_latch_on_ram_holds,
record_axis_is_latch_on_l_holds). The relation carries no cost: a machine
that multiplies unbounded numbers in one step and a Turing machine differ in cost,
not in records.

   Plain reading. The base is free. Over any base, the record axis has one form: a latch


                                           14
The Thiele Machine                                            Devon Thiele • September 2026


    on an event. Which event is the pointer question.

The proof of the factorization is short. Its content is in what the entry asks for: hid-
den state is allowed, and only a record that never switches off and is driven by the
computation is required. The latch form follows from those two; it is not assumed.

The third is about this exact machine. Is this particular VM state space the only one,
up to renaming? I don’t claim that anywhere. No theorem needs it. The separating
witnesses (Section 14’s graph pair and the irrecoverability theorems above) stand on
their own however the second question comes out. They don’t need it, and it doesn’t
need them.

So the first is proved, for a fixed schedule. The third isn’t claimed. The second, in
the form the words suggest, is false. Up to the price schedule it holds for machines
that run the VM and record certification, and which reading counts as the record is
a second free choice. Over any base, a record driven by the computation that never
switches off is a latch on one event.

What this project is not. It is not a claim that I solved P vs NP. It is not a theory of
everything. It is not a claim that computation is physics or that physics is computation.
The CHSH algebraic result is pure math. The Landauer connection is motivation. That
is the full extent of the physics content here. The rest is bookkeeping.

A note before the technical content, on what I do and do not know about prior
work. I started this with no background in computer science, math, physics, quantum
mechanics, hardware, or category theory. None. I didn’t know what a Turing machine
was. I didn’t know what Coq was. I built this alone. Nobody mentored me or walked
me through the field. The only person who has touched this work in any way is
a friend who taught me how to use GitHub and set up an IDE. Every name, every
comparison, every paper I went looking for, came from me searching on my own. So
treat what follows as a list of labels I reached for, not a literature review. Where I have
mismatched one against the real thing, tell me, because that is a correction I can use.

What I compare each time is what a result is actually about. Landauer’s bound is
about heat: a physical cost, under a thermodynamic model. No-free-lunch is about
averages over a whole spread of problems. A2 is a floor on one kind of step, the step
that flips a designated certification reading on. Exact pricing adds a second rule on
top of that floor: never charge more. So the comparisons below are about the shape
of each result. They are not claims about who got somewhere first. They don’t show
certification is the only event worth pricing, either. The two sources I lean on hardest
are Landauer, “Irreversibility and Heat Generation in the Computing Process” (1961),
and Wolpert–Macready, “No Free Lunch Theorems for Optimization” (1997).

Here are the labels I went and looked into. Each entry says what I understood the
label to mean and why I do not think Thiele reduces to it. Each is a claim inviting


                                            15
The Thiele Machine                                           Devon Thiele • September 2026


correction. If the comparison is wrong, tell me.

   • Linear logic (Girard). What I read: a kind of logic where assumptions are con-
     sumed when you use them, like physical resources. Compiled into modern
     programming languages, it shows up as type rules that say “this value can only
     be used once.” My reading of why Thiele is not a restatement: linear logic con-
     strains what the program can write down. Thiele’s A2 rule constrains what the
     machine’s step function does. Same intent. Different layer.
   • Proof-carrying code (Necula). What I read: when one machine downloads code
     from another, the code can carry a small proof that the host can check before
     running it. The proof is data accompanying the program. My reading: Thiele’s
     cost-on-certification is enforced by the machine itself, not by a proof bundled
     with the program. The program does not carry receipts. The machine produces
     them. Foundational PCC (Appel) is closer in spirit: proofs about a small trusted
     base, not certificates bundled with the program. I haven’t read enough Appel to
     say more.
   • Session types (Honda). What I read: type systems that constrain how two or
     more concurrent programs talk to each other so the conversation cannot go off-
     script. My reading: Thiele tracks structural facts a single computation has paid
     for. It does not constrain inter-program communication. Different problem.
   • Bennett’s reversibility argument. What I read: Charles Bennett (1973) showed
     that you can in principle organise any computation so it does not erase informa-
     tion mid-stream. Operations that throw information away are the irreversible
     ones. Everything else can be undone. My reading: in the 51-opcode realisation,
     CERTIFY and MORPH_ASSERT and EMIT and REVEAL, among others, are
     charged even at δ = 0, a floor no program can waive, while the rest are free at
     δ = 0. Positive cost is the larger set, since any instruction can carry a voluntary
     δ; the mandatory floor is the part I put there on purpose. The shape matches.
     The content does not: the floor is an accounting rule, not a proof that those op-
     erations are logically or physically irreversible. The history-lift counterexample
     in Section 19 shows why transition injectivity and charge are separate questions.
     The dollars-and-joules calibration is a separate named hypothesis.
   • Landauer’s bound. What I read: Rolf Landauer (1961) argued that erasing one
     bit of information must release at least kT ln 2 of heat into the environment
     around the chip. The expression means Boltzmann’s constant times temperature
     times the natural log of 2. My reading: this is the physical-units side of Bennett’s
     argument. In Thiele the structural side, the substrate tracking each positive-cost
     transition via µ, is proved. The conversion to joules is a named hypothesis (see
     Section 19). I treat Landauer’s claim as motivation, not as something the proofs
     rest on. Motivation pays no bills in Coq.
   • No Free Lunch (Wolpert and Macready, 1997). What I read: averaged over


                                           16
The Thiele Machine                                            Devon Thiele • September 2026


      every possible problem, no search method beats blind guessing. Whatever an
      algorithm wins on one set of problems it gives back on another. My reading: this
      is the same “structure isn’t free” intuition, but it lives as an average over a whole
      problem space, and the cost is refundable. You buy an edge on your problems by
      spending prior assumptions, and you pay for it somewhere else. A2 charges one
      commitment event in one run, with no refund anywhere. Same family. Different
      shape: an average, not a per-step floor.
   • Kolmogorov complexity (Kolmogorov 1965; Solomonoff and Chaitin reached
     it independently around then). What I read: the amount of structure in an
     object is the length of the shortest program that prints it. Most objects have no
     short description, so you cannot squeeze below that floor. My reading: same
     “structure isn’t free” intuition, but it is a static property of a finished object.
     No steps. No moment of paying. A2 charges the moment a running machine
     commits to a certificate. Same family. Different shape: a property of a string, not
     a law of a step.
   • Cook-Levin and P vs NP (Cook 1971, Levin 1973). What I read: checking a
     solution can be cheap while finding one looks expensive. Whether finding is
     genuinely harder than checking is the open P-vs-NP question. Cook-Levin is the
     theorem that SAT is NP-complete, not a proof that search is hard. My reading:
     same find-versus-check intuition, but it is an asymptotic, worst-case, still-open
     statement about how difficulty grows with problem size. A2 charges a constant
     on the one commitment step in a concrete run, whether finding it was easy or
     hard. Same family. Different shape: an open scaling gap, not a per-step floor. I
     am not claiming anything about P vs NP; see “What this project is not.”
   • Rovelli’s cost of choosing (Rovelli 2024, The Thermodynamic Cost of Choosing).
     What I read: committing to one branch of a binary choice at temperature T
     dissipates at least kT ln 2. That is the same floor Landauer found for erasure,
     now charged on the act of deciding rather than the act of forgetting. My reading:
     this is the closest existing thing to A2’s claim. It charges a commitment, not
     a finished object or an average. But it is physical: indexed to a temperature,
     living in thermodynamics. A2 charges the commitment in a substrate, with
     no temperature and no physics, blind to how the witness was found. Closest
     neighbour. Not the same chair.
   • CerCo (certified cost annotations through a verified C compiler). What I read:
     take a C program, run it through a compiler that itself has been formally proved
     correct, and attach cost annotations to the source program. The compiler pre-
     serves those annotations all the way down to machine code. My reading:
     CerCo’s cost lives as decoration on the source code that survives compilation.
     Thiele’s cost lives in the step rule of the abstract machine. The path down to
     hardware is proved as far as the Kami model, under stated premises, and tested
     past it. Different mechanism. Different layer.


                                            17
The Thiele Machine                                          Devon Thiele • September 2026


   • Kami-RISC-V. What I read: a verified processor design written in Coq using
     the Kami framework that targets the RISC-V instruction set. RISC-V is an open-
     source CPU architecture, a published list of instructions any chip designer can
     implement. My reading: I use the same Coq-to-Kami-to-Bluespec pipeline that
     the Kami-RISC-V work pioneered. The instruction set is mine. The cost disci-
     pline is mine. The proofs were built for this machine and exist nowhere else. The
     toolchain is shared. Nothing else is.
   • NPA hierarchy (Navascués-Pironio-Acín). What I read: a way of bounding what
     kinds of correlations between two separated experiments are achievable using
     quantum mechanics. The bound takes the form of a sequence of polynomial
     inequalities, each tighter than the last. The bounds are usually checked by
     an external numerical solver. My reading: Thiele puts one of those bounds
     inside the machine’s own step rule. A chain of Coq theorems proves that when
     the integer check CHSH_LASSERT runs passes, the specified zero-marginal Q1
     NPA matrix is PSD (Section 18). That direction is the one proved. The matrix
     condition itself is proved equivalent to three polynomial inequalities. A second
     chain carries it one level up, to Q1+AB , once for each of the four opcodes in that
     family. That is two levels of NPA. The tower goes higher. The machine enforces
     the bound at the step itself. Nobody has to check it afterward from the outside.
   • Lawvere’s fixed-point theorem (1969). What I read: in a cartesian closed category,
     if a map from A onto the functions A → Y hits every function, then every map
     from Y to itself has a fixed point. Cantor, Gödel, Tarski, and Turing’s halting
     argument are all instances. My reading: the diagonal in Section 10 is one more
     instance. Nothing about the diagonal is mine. What I added is the packaging as
     an interface, and one model, L, where the recursion theorem it needs is proved
     rather than assumed (Section 10.4).
                                                                                     √
   • Tsirelson (1980) and Landau (1988). What I read: Tsirelson proved the 2 2
     bound and characterized which two-outcome correlators quantum mechanics
     can produce: exactly the ones that are inner products of unit vectors. Landau
     gave the explicit condition for the two-setting case. My reading: the column-
     contractivity biconditional and the elliptope results in Section 18 are their mathe-
     matics, rederived in Coq for one slice and turned into an integer check an opcode
     runs. The characterization is theirs. The step that refuses to advance on a tally
     outside it is mine.
   • Bennett (1982), on Maxwell’s demon. What I read: measuring can be done
     without erasing anything, so it can be free; what costs is erasing the record to
     make room. My reading: Section 9.7 checks exactly that on the logic, and the
     permanent-certificate theorem is the certificate-specific version of it. Same idea.
     Mine is stated for every encoding of a permanent certificate on a finite state
     space whose merges are priced, and machine-checked.
   • Maroney (2009) and Sagawa and Ueda (2008 onward). What I read: logical irre-


                                          18
The Thiele Machine                                            Devon Thiele • September 2026


      versibility and thermodynamic cost come apart; the heat Landauer’s principle
      demands depends on the actual distribution over states. For a state already
      known, the entropy bound supplies no positive heat floor. My reading: the
      known-state boundary in Section 9.5 is their point, restated for certificates. I am
      not adding physics there. I am adding the link to the certifying step.
   • Zurek’s quantum Darwinism (2009). What I read: the classical-looking facts
     are the ones the environment keeps many independent copies of. Pointer ob-
     servables win because they are redundantly recorded. My reading: the pointer-
     observable conjecture of Section 26 borrows that shape for computation. It is an
     analogy, and I have not shown it is more than one.
   • Hashcash and proof of work (Dwork and Naor 1992; Back 1997). What I read:
     make a sender spend computation that a receiver can check cheaply, so estab-
     lishing something costs. My reading: same instinct, different object. Proof of
     work checks a witness against a difficulty target; that check does not measure
     the effort spent on a particular successful attempt. A2 charges one ledger unit at
     the step that certifies and verifies nothing about effort.
   • Quantitative information flow (Smith 2009). What I read: measure how much an
     observer learns about a secret from what it sees, in bits of min-entropy. My read-
     ing: the irrecoverability theorems rule out exact decoding from named windows.
     They do not establish zero information leakage: a window can reveal part of a
     secret without determining it. The observer knowledge in Section 9.7 describes
     which initial states remain consistent with the observed trace; no min-entropy
     leakage theorem is supplied there. QIF measures what an observer learns. It
     doesn’t price committing to it.
   • Graded monads and graded types (Katsumata 2014; Orchard, Liepelt, and Eades
     2019). What I read: a type system can carry a cost as a grade on each computation,
     and the grades compose as the computations do. My reading: µ is a grade
     carried in the state by the step rule rather than in a type. How close that is, an
     embedding or something weaker, is a question I have not settled.

I read enough of each to convince myself Thiele is not a restatement. I cannot say that
with the confidence of someone who has actually read the field. The only things I will
tell you with confidence are the ones where the argument actually closes. Structure
is state in this model. In the VM build it has its own opcodes to reach it. Fix the cost
schedule and the starting value, and exactly one ledger fits: µ. mu_is_initial_
monotone proves that on every state the machine can reach. One local rule, that a
certification flip costs at least one, forces a cost onto every run that starts uncertified
and ends certified, on any abstract certification system at all. That is universal_
nfi_any_substrate. Two states can agree on every classical field and still have
different graphs (Section 14). The classical view cannot see that difference. A passing
CHSH gate implies positive-semidefiniteness of the specified NPA matrix, and the



                                            19
The Thiele Machine                                        Devon Thiele • September 2026


algebra gets there without any quantum input. The argument is the part I can defend.
The proof corpus is where I checked I hadn’t fooled myself. The literature placement
is the part where I am asking for help.
