the mandatory-floor class. So at that step: µafter = µbefore + S(δ) ≥ µbefore + 1. And
because µ never decreases (each instruction adds a non-negative cost), µbefore ≥ µinitial .
Therefore µfinal ≥ µafter ≥ µbefore + 1 ≥ µinitial + 1.

That comes out of the step and cost rules, by case analysis and induction over the trace.
In this model a certification transition can’t be free. Whether the certified thing is true
is a separate question. A paid flag proves nothing on its own, and a checker can be
sound whatever the charging convention happens to be.

Cost alone isn’t depth, either. A machine can spend real resources checking a claim that
turns out to be shallow. LASSERT doesn’t count as structural certification just because
a formula parses and one satisfying assignment exists. It also needs a non-tautology
witness: one satisfying assignment and one falsifying assignment. Under checker
soundness, that proves the encoded formula can be satisfied and isn’t a tautology. It
doesn’t, by itself, make the falsifying valuation a state in the current prior feasible
set, and it doesn’t make the formula a sound factorization or decomposition of the
problem being run. Those take their own representation and semantic proofs. The
receipt proves what was spent. The witness proves the stated formula property and
nothing more.

7 The µ ledger
µ (mu) is the global structural cost ledger of the Thiele substrate. One counter, carried
by every state. Not the whole substrate. The part that keeps receipts.

7.1 What mu is
µ is a single natural number riding along on every substrate state (a natural number
being a non-negative integer, 0, 1, 2, . . .; the notation cheat sheet is in Section A). It
starts at 0 and it never goes down, and that second half is the whole character of the
thing. A counter that can fall is an accountant you can lean on to revise the books. A
counter that only ever climbs is a fact about history: it says how much has been spent,


                                            61
The Thiele Machine                                              Devon Thiele • September 2026


and it cannot be talked into saying less. µ is the second kind.

In the 51-opcode realisation, every instruction carries a cost parameter (δ) in its encod-
ing. The VM adds that cost to µ when the instruction executes. Here is how δ is used
in practice:

For pure compute programs, meaning programs that do arithmetic, load and store
values, and jump, every instruction carries δ = 0. The concrete zero-cost comparison
case, blind_program, has this property: every instruction sits at δ = 0. The proof
that its total µ-cost is zero is a direct calculation. For these programs, µ stays at 0
unless a positive-cost instruction fires.

For certification and revelation instructions (CERTIFY, LASSERT, MORPH_ASSERT,
EMIT, REVEAL, LJOIN, READ_PORT, and the five-opcode CHSH_LASSERT check fam-
ily): the cost is at least 1, regardless of δ. Even δ = 0 still costs 1. The positive floor is
part of this instruction schedule.

CERTIFY sets a persistent flag and charges S(δ) = δ + 1. The first false-to-true transi-
tion is the event A2 prices. Running it again still costs under the VM schedule. A flag
that stays set doesn’t prove anything was physically erased, either. An implementation
can keep the old state somewhere else.

One is the smallest positive whole number, so a mandatory floor of one on top of δ
gives δ + 1. A2 allows more than that. The substitution theorems pin down exact
unit pricing relative to how many flips happen. They don’t pin down the whole VM
schedule, and they don’t pick a physical unit.

More things to watch doesn’t mean a bigger bill, either. joint_floor_is_lea
st proves that the smallest charge satisfying a finite family of unit event floors is
one whenever any event fires, and zero otherwise. One unit can cover several floors
at once. Two independent yes/no coordinates are a concrete example. If you want
their costs to add, you need an accounting rule that actually makes them add. Being
independent doesn’t do it.

Throughout this document, S(n) means n + 1. The S stands for “successor” in the
basic math sense: the successor of n is the next natural number after n. So S(0) = 1,
S(1) = 2, and so on. S(δ) = δ + 1 is always at least 1. EMIT, REVEAL, and READ_PORT
go further: their cost also grows with the information content they carry. An EMIT of
one ASCII character (which is 8 bits, see Section A) with δ = 0 costs 8 + 1 = 9. Ten
characters costs 80 + 1 = 81. Emitting more costs more. The price tracks the bits, every
time, and there’s no rate to negotiate.

For everything outside that positive-cost class, including ADD, LOAD, STORE, JUMP,
PNEW, PSPLIT, PMERGE, MORPH, COMPOSE, and the rest, the VM charges exactly δ,
which can be 0. Here is why these are allowed to be free.

The schedule lets module operations run at δ = 0. That’s a design choice. It isn’t

                                             62
The Thiele Machine                                           Devon Thiele • September 2026


derived from thermodynamics. A zero-cost PNEW can change the graph, so “every
observable structural change costs something” would be false for this VM.

Building graph structure and asserting it are charged differently here. Reading any-
thing physical into that would take a model of which states carry information, where
operations begin and end, what history is kept, and what dissipates. Opcode names
and graph changes don’t tell you any of that.

µ grows whenever instruction_cost is positive. The certification/revelation class
is forced to be positive even when its encoded δ is 0. Other instructions can remain
free by carrying δ = 0.

Here is the complete cost schedule:


  Instruction class                   Cost = instruction_cost
 Zero-floor operations (ADD,          δ   (can be 0)
 LOAD, JUMP, PNEW, MORPH, . . . )
 Certification floor (CERTIFY,        S(δ) = δ + 1
 MORPH_ASSERT, LJOIN, the
 CHSH_LASSERT family)
 EMIT (emit payload as receipt-       |payload|bits + S(δ)
 visible trace event)
 REVEAL (disclose bits from a         bits + S(δ)
 module)
 READ_PORT (read bits from in-        bits + S(δ)
 put port)
 LASSERT (validate formula and        flen × 8 + S(δ)
 witnesses)

The |payload|bits notation means “the number of bits in the payload.” A one-byte
ASCII character contains 8 bits. A ten-byte string contains 80 bits.

For LASSERT: flen is the number of formula units in the encoded formula. Each unit
is one memory word. The formula header declares how many words follow. The VM
charges 8 per unit. The factor 8 is a pricing convention of the schedule. It is not a
theorem that each unit is a byte or that the charge counts physical information.

The proof that µ is always exactly µbefore + instruction_cost for each step:

   Plain reading. Look at each of the 51 opcodes one at a time. Every apply rule writes
   µ as the old µ plus the cost. The cost term is exactly what instruction_cost
   returns. That includes the error paths. Failing an instruction costs the same as
   succeeding at it, because the cost is on the attempt. The outcome doesn’t change
   it. No opcode escapes this. Every one of the 51 lands in the same equation.



                                           63
The Thiele Machine                                           Devon Thiele • September 2026


Theorem 7.1 (µ-Conservation). For any VM state s and VM instruction i:
(vm_apply s i ).µ = s.µ + instruction_cost i.

Proof. Case analysis over the 51 opcode constructors of vm_instruction. For each
constructor i, the apply rule has the form

                            (vm_apply s i ).µ = s.µ + ci ,

where ci is the cost term written in the apply rule. Compare to instruction_cost:
for zero-floor opcodes ci = δ; for certification-floor opcodes (CERTIFY,
MORPH_ASSERT, LJOIN, and the CHSH_LASSERT family, its four Q1+ AB variants in-
cluded) ci = S(δ); for EMIT ci = |payload|bits + S(δ); for REVEAL and READ_PORT
ci = bits + S(δ); for LASSERT ci = flen × 8 + S(δ). In every case ci matches the
corresponding case of instruction_cost by definition. The error sub-cases (e.g.,
MORPH_ASSERT on a missing morphism, CHSH_TRIAL with bad bits, LASSERT length
mismatch) follow the same cost rule: the trap or err-latch only changes the PC
and the error flag; the µ update is the same. So (vm_apply s i ).µ = s.µ + ci =
s.µ + instruction_cost(i ) for every i.

This is not a monotonicity claim. It is an equality. µ goes up by exactly the cost of the
instruction, always, for every instruction, in every case including error cases. There is
no backdoor.

Don’t read too much into that equality. It means the ledger is exact about what was
spent. It doesn’t mean every unit spent bought something deep. On this substrate,
real semantic gain is a higher bar than spending. It needs a narrowing witness on top
of the ledger entry.

7.2 Why mu is the unique ledger for its schedule
Here is the stronger claim. µ is not just a valid cost measure for this 51-opcode re-
alisation. Once the schedule is fixed, it is the unique one. Any other cost function
that starts at zero and tracks the per-instruction cost schedule exactly must equal µ on
every reachable VM state. Not “isomorphic” in some abstract sense. Literally equal,
value by value, on every reachable state.

   Plain reading. M agrees with µ at the start (both zero). On every step, M and µ
   move by the same amount (M by hypothesis (b), µ by µ-Conservation). Induction
   closes it. No other accounting can match the cost schedule at every step, start at
   zero, and still disagree with µ on a reachable VM state. Given the schedule, the
   count is forced.


Theorem 7.2 (µ-Initiality). Let M be any function from VM states to natural numbers
satisfying: (a) M assigns zero to the initial VM state, M(init_state) = 0; and (b)
M tracks the cost schedule exactly: for every VM state s and every VM instruction i,

                                           64
The Thiele Machine                                            Devon Thiele • September 2026


M (vm_apply s i ) = M (s) + instruction_cost(i ). Then M equals µ on every reach-
able VM state. (Monotonicity, the property that M (s′ ) ≥ M(s) when s′ is reachable from s,
follows from (b) because instruction_cost is a non-negative natural number. You do
not need to state monotonicity separately.)

Proof. Induction on the reachability witness for s. A reachable VM state is either
init_state itself or vm_apply s′ i for some reachable s′ and some VM instruction i.

Base case (s = init_state). By hypothesis (a), M (init_state) = 0. By defini-
tion of the initial state in the kernel, init_state.µ = 0. So M(init_state) =
init_state.µ.

Step case (s = vm_apply s′ i for reachable s′ ). By hypothesis (b),

                      M(s) = M (s′ ) + instruction_cost(i ).

By the induction hypothesis on s′ , M(s′ ) = s′ .µ. By µ-Conservation,

             s.µ = (vm_apply s′ i ).µ = s′ .µ + instruction_cost(i ).

Substituting: M(s) = s′ .µ + instruction_cost(i ) = s.µ.

In plain language: given the cost schedule, there’s no other way to keep the books.
Charge S(δ) for the certification-floor opcodes, |payload|bits + S(δ) for EMIT, and δ for
everything else, and µ is the only function on VM states that starts at zero and follows
the schedule. The schedule itself was a design choice: the decision that CERTIFY
costs S(δ) and ADD costs δ. It isn’t arbitrary, though, and that’s the part worth slowing
down on. Two more results sit under it. The first is about pricing. A charge meets
the certification floor exactly when it covers every flip, and it meets the floor without
ever overcharging exactly when it charges on the flips and nowhere else, one unit each
(a2_equal_trust_substitution_payoff). Hold trust fixed and the only exact
rule left is A2’s own. The second is about what can’t be dropped. A window that
forgets µ can’t answer questions about µ, and a window that forgets the certification
flag can’t answer questions about certification. The full window answers both (P_f
ull_complete_neither_mu_nor_cert_droppable). Neither one is redundant.
A law that lives in the step isn’t a check you run afterward and hope you got right.
Uniqueness given the schedule is the bookkeeping. Pricing and irredundancy are why
the bookkeeping has this shape. No competing count can match the schedule, start at
zero, and land on a different total, and there’s nowhere left for that freedom to hide.

7.3 The LASSERT cost formula
The most important cost formula in the VM is for LASSERT, the opcode that validates
a logical formula and its witnesses:




                                            65
The Thiele Machine                                            Devon Thiele • September 2026




                          ∆µLASSERT = flen × 8 + S(mu_delta)

flen is the formula-unit count the instruction declares, and the declaration drives the
bill: eight priced bits per declared unit, a pricing convention. The per-step mandatory
charge is always at least 1, no matter how cheap the unit-count feels. What keeps the
declaration honest is the header check below: the VM reads the count the formula’s
own in-memory header carries, and no validation succeeds unless the two agree.

What makes this the one to watch is where the bill gets keyed. The operand drives
the charge, but no successful check ever underpays, because succeeding requires the
declared count to equal the count sitting in the formula’s own header. Eight bits for
every unit, plus the floor of at least 1 you pay for the act of checking at all, and the act
of checking is not up for negotiation. So a two-unit formula lands at 16 + 1 = 17, and
there is no posture you can strike to talk it down: the in-memory header the checker
reads gives the count, and a declaration that disagrees with it never validates anything.
Declaring a smaller count does not shrink the formula; it only gets you caught. That
is the whole reason you cannot declare fewer units than that header carries.

That minimum is relative to the stated formula-length charging rule, flen · 8 + S(cost),
as lassert_honest_mu_cost states it for a non-trapping step. lassert_cost
_from_component_floors and lassert_cost_is_its_formula formalize a
separate decomposition: a state-reduction term plus description bits. It isn’t a lower
bound on every possible way of validating or representing a formula.

What this buys you: the trace pays for presenting and checking a constraint package,
no more, no less. It does not, by itself, guarantee the constraint is meaningful. You
could present a formula that is always true, a tautology, and the ledger would still
charge you the full bill. The VM closes this separately via the dual-witness require-
ment: see the LASSERT opcode description in Section 12.

The obvious thing to try, and where it runs out. The programmer declares flen in the
instruction encoding, so the first thing I wanted to know was: what stops them from
writing flen = 0 for a huge formula and paying only S(0) = 1? It’s the cheat I’d reach
for first.

The VM catches it. When LASSERT runs, the VM reads the formula’s actual encoded-
unit count from the formula header in memory and compares it to whatever flen
the instruction claimed. If they do not match, the VM traps (Section A: routes exe-
cution to a fixed error address): the program counter jumps to LASSERT_TRAP_PC,
which is 0xF00 in hexadecimal, decimal 3840. The error flag is set. The check does
not succeed. This trap-to-fixed-address behaviour belongs to LASSERT and to the
CHSH_LASSERT check family that inherits its discipline; every other error in the VM
simply sets vm_err to true and continues advancing the program counter by 1. The
checks are special because a failed check of this kind is a serious integrity violation. It


                                            66
The Thiele Machine                                                      Devon Thiele • September 2026


isn’t the kind of error you just step past. The µ cost is still charged: that detail matters
because failed checks are not free. The Coq lemmas lassert_honest_cost and l
assert_honest_mu_cost prove that if LASSERT completes without trapping, the
declared count equals the in-memory header count, and the µ charge is that count
times 8 plus S(cost).

8 No Free Insight: the first cost law
Section 4 ended on one sentence: the gain has a location, the location has a price, and
the price is never zero. This section is that sentence as a theorem, twice. Once for any
system at all, and once for this machine.

8.1 The abstract versions

   Plain reading. The cert predicate is off at the start, on at the end. So somewhere a
   step flipped it. Whichever step did the flipping costs at least 1 by the single-step
   rule. Every other step costs at least 0 because costs are non-negative naturals. Sum
   them up: at least 1. Empty trace can’t do the flipping at all, so it’s vacuous. That’s
   the whole argument. The induction just makes precise the idea that the flip has to
   happen somewhere, and wherever it happens it pays.


Theorem 8.1 (Universal No Free Certification). Let C be any abstract computational
system with:

    • a set of states;
    • a set of step labels;
    • a step rule (given a state and a step label, the rule produces the next state);
    • a cost function (each step label has a non-negative integer cost);
    • a certification predicate (a yes/no test on states, with at least one “no” state and at least
      one “yes” state).

Premise (A2, the single-step cost rule): for every state s and step label i, if cert(s) = false and
cert(step(s, i )) = true, then cost(i ) ≥ 1.

Conclusion: any trace of step labels t = [i1 , i2 , . . . , in ] that starts at state s0 with cert(s0 ) =
false and ends at state sn with cert(sn ) = true has total cost ∑nk=1 cost(ik ) ≥ 1.

Proof. Induction on the trace t.

Base case (t = []). Then sn = s0 , so cert(sn ) = cert(s0 ) = false. But the hypothesis says
cert(sn ) = true. Contradiction; the empty-trace case is vacuous.

Step case (t = i :: rest). Let s1 = step(s0 , i ) be the state after the first step label. Case-
split on cert(s1 ).


                                                   67
The Thiele Machine                                             Devon Thiele • September 2026


   • Subcase A: cert(s1 ) = true. The first step label flipped the certification predicate
     from false to true in one step. By the A2 premise, cost(i ) ≥ 1. The total cost is
     cost(i ) + ∑ j∈rest cost( j) ≥ 1 + 0 = 1, since every individual cost is a non-negative
     natural.
   • Subcase B: cert(s1 ) = false. Then the rest of the trace must do the flip-
     ping: cert(run(rest, s1 )) = true with cert(s1 ) = false. Apply the induc-
     tion hypothesis at (rest, s1 ) to get ∑ j∈rest cost( j) ≥ 1. The total trace cost is
     cost(i ) + ∑ j∈rest cost( j) ≥ 0 + 1 = 1.

Either subcase gives total trace cost ≥ 1.

This theorem does not know about registers, memory, partitions, morphisms, CHSH
trials, or the 51-opcode ISA. It says: once a model has a yes/no certification test and
the one-step certification transition is priced, trace-level non-free certification falls out
by induction over the trace, no further premises required. If that one-step premise
fails, the model permits free certification by definition. If the model has no certification
predicate, it cannot talk about structural entitlement internally.

And don’t read “that’s the whole argument” as “the premise is the whole content.”
The single implication is one line from the pricing rule, yes. It is an accounting law,
and I call it one. It isn’t a law pulled from nowhere, though. The nearest older rule
is Landauer’s erasure accounting, and the two meet at an exact place. A trusted
erasure-accounting law, the same trust posture, certifies at total cost zero when no
erasure is reported (commitment_cost_not_reducible_to_erasure_cost).
Its one step sends both states to certified. That is a merge, and it goes unreported.
Let the erasure flag report every merging instruction, and on a finite machine with a
permanent certificate the erasure law charges the certification flip at least one (honest
_erasure_accounting_implies_a2). Same bill as A2 (Section 9.1). So A2 is what
an erasure law that reports every merge says about the step that certifies, wherever
memory is finite and certificates last. Where Landauer’s rule isn’t in force, A2 still
prices the flip. The VM is that case: its free JUMP merges program counters, and its
CERTIFY, itself a merge, still pays (vm_prices_certifying_merge_leaves_ot
hers_free). And if the goal is to price exactly the certification flips, never less and
never more, a charge on any other predicate misses that goal (substitution_tes
t_rejects_non_a2_exact_substitute). That uniqueness is relative to the goal.
Whether certification is the event that deserves the price is the conjecture of Section 26.
A2 is a rule I chose. On finite hardware with certificates that last, it is also the bill a
merge-reporting erasure law sends for that step.

   Plain reading. This is the abstract theorem pointed at the 51-opcode machine. Its
   two concrete certification channels, csr_cert_addr and vm_certified, both
   cost something at the step that turns them on. And the predicate-strengthening
   reading falls out of the structure-addition theorem.


                                             68
The Thiele Machine                                                 Devon Thiele • September 2026


Theorem 8.2 (Thiele No Free Insight). For the 51-opcode VM specifically, cert-channel
activation is positive-cost, and certified predicate strengthening has to pass through a structure-
addition event. The relevant certification/revelation steps all live in the positive-cost class.

Proof. Two parts.

Cert-channel activation requires positive cost. The Both-channels theorem below es-
tablishes that any trace activating either certification channel (csr_cert_addr
becoming nonzero or vm_certified becoming true) has ∆µ ≥ 1. The ac-
tual channel-writing cases are covered by the positive-cost cert/revelation class:
CERTIFY, MORPH_ASSERT, EMIT, REVEAL, LJOIN, LASSERT, READ_PORT, and the
CHSH_LASSERT family. The class is deliberately broader than literal field writers;
every member has instruction_cost ≥ S(δ) ≥ 1, so the lower bound holds.

Certified predicate strengthening requires a structure-addition event. By hypothesis the trace
ends certified for a stronger predicate via the supra-cert channel (csr_cert_addr
nonzero). The Strengthening-requires-structure-addition theorem (Section 5) gives the
structure-addition event directly.

This is the VM-level version. The kernel proves the two concrete cert channels cannot
be activated for free, by case analysis over all 51 opcodes, then adds the predicate-
strengthening layer: once a stronger accepted predicate is tied to has_supra_cert,
the trace must contain a structure-addition event somewhere in it.

8.2 The law is the potential method
Cost reasoning has old tools, and I checked the law against four of them.

The first is cost semantics. Danner and Licata use a monadic translation to carry cost
with a program’s result; graded monads (Katsumata; Orchard, Liepelt, and Eades)
track effects through grades. Running a trace in the writer monad over the natural
numbers returns the final state and the trace’s total cost (run_writer_is_run_and_
cost). This identifies the trace ledger with a writer cost. With costs set per instruction,
the cost of a run is also its grade, fixed before the run in the type of the computation
(run_graded_is_run), which is what a graded monad asks. None of this embeds
those authors’ full semantics or type systems.

The second is a lower-bound potential argument. Give every state a potential. If every
step’s cost plus the change in potential is never negative, then any run costs at least
the drop in potential. Now pick the potential that is one on an uncertified state and
zero on a certified one. Standard upper-bound AARA uses the opposite inequality:
initial potential covers cost plus final potential. The result here is not an equivalence
to its affine type system.


Theorem 8.3 (A2 is the potential method, a2_iff_nonnegative_amortized_co



                                                69
The Thiele Machine                                           Devon Thiele • September 2026


st). For any step function, cost, and yes/no reading, A2 holds exactly when every step has
non-negative amortized cost under that potential.


No Free Insight is then the potential method’s own bound: a run from uncertified
to certified drops the potential by one, so it costs at least one (nfi_by_potential).
Every CertificationSystem is an instance (certification_system_is_pot
ential_method).

The upper-bound direction says something too. Take AARA’s condition with the same
potential: the potential before a step pays for its cost and the potential after. A2 and
that condition hold together exactly when every certifying step costs one, every other
step costs zero, and nothing ever loses its certificate (a2_and_aara_iff_exact). So
the upper bound adds exact pricing and permanence to the floor.

The last is linear logic’s reading, where a resource is used once. Under A2 a trace
certifies at most as many times as its total cost (flips_le_cost). Each certification
uses up a unit, and a unit is not used twice.

So the law isn’t a new kind of cost reasoning, and I’m glad to say so. It is a lower-bound
potential argument, pointed at the certification reading. What the rest of this book
adds is about that reading, not the method: Section 9.4 shows that on the VM’s two
named windows no price computed from the observed transition prices certification
exactly, and a potential read off the window gives a price of that kind. This observation
does not establish a separation from the cited frameworks.

8.3 The specific versions
For the 51-opcode VM concretely, there are several specific versions. The two
certification channels I mentioned in Section 6 are the certificate-address register
(csr_cert_addr) and the top-level flag (vm_certified). Both are defined pre-
cisely in Section 11, register by register.

   Plain reading. If you see csr_cert_addr move from zero to nonzero, the proof
   catches that step inside a cost-positive guardrail class. That class is broader than
   the direct CSR writers, but broad is fine here: every member costs at least 1. The
   field cannot flip for free.


Theorem 8.4 (No Free Certification, single step). If s.vm_csrs.csr_cert_addr = 0
and (vm_apply s i ).vm_csrs.csr_cert_addr ̸= 0, then instruction_cost(i ) ≥
1.

Proof. Case analysis over the 51 opcode constructors.          The Coq proof uses
cert_addr_setterb: if csr_cert_addr changes from 0 to nonzero, the instruc-
tion must fall inside that cost-positive class. The class includes instr_morph_a
ssert, instr_emit, instr_reveal, instr_ljoin, and instr_lassert. It is


                                           70
The Thiele Machine                                           Devon Thiele • September 2026


a deliberately broad guardrail class, not a list of literal CSR writers. In the current
vm_apply semantics, the direct CSR-writing success case is instr_morph_assert.
For the theorem, the required fact is cost: every member of cert_addr_setterb has
instruction_cost ≥ 1, with instr_emit and instr_reveal higher because
they also charge payload bits.

   Plain reading. Single-step lifts to trace-level. The universal theorem does the work.


Theorem 8.5 (No Free Certification, trace level). If a trace starts at a state with
csr_cert_addr = 0 and ends at a state with csr_cert_addr ̸= 0, then the trace’s
total µ cost is ≥ 1.

Proof. Instantiate the universal theorem above with C = the 51-opcode VM; cert(s) :=
(s.vm_csrs.csr_cert_addr ̸= 0); step = vm_apply; cost = instruction_cost.
The A2 premise is exactly the single-step theorem above.

   Plain reading. Same shape as the other channel. Only one opcode (CERTIFY) can
   flip the top-level flag. It charges ≥ 1. The universal theorem lifts that to the trace.


Theorem 8.6 (No Free Certification via CERTIFY). If a trace starts at a state with
vm_certified = false and ends at a state with vm_certified = true, then the trace’s
total µ cost is ≥ 1.

Proof. Case analysis over the 51 opcodes shows that vm_certified flips from
false to true only when i is an instr_certify constructor; every other opcode
leaves it unchanged. The CERTIFY apply rule charges S(δ) ≥ 1. So the single-
step instance of A2 holds for this channel. Instantiate the universal theorem with
cert(s) := s.vm_certified, otherwise as above.

   Plain reading. Two channels, one master claim. Whichever channel got activated,
   the corresponding single-step rule fired and the universal theorem lifted it to the
   trace. These are the two certification channels the kernel defines, and the theorem
   covers both.


Theorem 8.7 (Both channels). For ANY transition of either certification channel from
absent to present, whether the certificate-address register csr_cert_addr or the top-level
flag vm_certified, µ grows by at least 1 over the trace.

Proof. Suppose the trace either flips csr_cert_addr from 0 to nonzero, or flips
vm_certified from false to true (or both). In the first case the trace-level
theorem (csr_cert_addr) applies. In the second case the trace-level theorem
(vm_certified) applies. The disjunction is exhaustive over “cert-channel activa-
tion,” so µ grows by ≥ 1 in either case.

                                           71
The Thiele Machine                                           Devon Thiele • September 2026


Theorem 8.7 is the one I actually reach for downstream. When something later in
the document needs “and certification was not free,” this is the line it cites, be-
cause it has already done the bookkeeping of asking which way the certification
came in and answered: doesn’t matter, both are priced. The certificate-address reg-
ister csr_cert_addr and the top-level flag vm_certified are not two separate
promises I have to keep track of; they collapse into one claim that holds no matter
which door a program walks through to call itself certified.

8.4 What this means in practice
Run any program on the kernel. Watch the µ counter. If the program starts with the
channel off, as the initial state does, and ends with vm_certified = true or with
csr_cert_addr nonzero, then somewhere in the trace, µ went up by at least 1. You
can audit the trace and find exactly which opcode paid the bill. There is no program
that can reach a certified state from scratch without paying for it.

That’s for a real run from the initial state. A certified flag there means the ledger went
up. The ledger can’t vouch for a state somebody else hands you, and it doesn’t prove
any proposition on its own.

9 Why A2 isn’t arbitrary: permanent certificates on finite hardware
A2 is a rule I chose. This section is about whether I had to. On a machine with finite
memory whose certificate lasts, the answer is close to yes. The step that certifies has to
merge states, and merging is what Landauer’s principle prices whenever the machine
could be in either state. The physics stays a named premise. The counting doesn’t.

9.1 A permanent certificate has to merge states
Here is the shape. A machine has finitely many states. Some are certified, and no step
ever un-certifies one. Now a step carries an uncertified state into the certified region.
The certified states were already there, and they can’t leave, so every certified landing
place is spoken for by one of them. The newcomer has to land where some certified
state also lands. Two states become one, and the step forgets which one it came from.

Forgetting like that is what Landauer’s principle charges for, with one condition that
matters. The principle prices the entropy a step removes from what the machine might
actually be holding. If the machine could be in either of the two merged states, the
merge removes entropy and costs heat. If it is already known to be in one of them,
nothing is lost and nothing is owed. So the premise here prices every merge at one or
more, which is Landauer’s bill in the worst case over what the machine might hold.
Under that premise, on a finite machine whose certificate can’t be revoked, A2 stops
being a rule I picked. It is what Landauer’s principle says about the one step that
certifies. Section 9.5 states the version that depends on the distribution exactly.

   Plain reading. The certified states form a closed room. A step that forgets nothing
   shuffles the people in the room among the room’s own seats, and a closed room

                                           72
The Thiele Machine                                                   Devon Thiele • September 2026


    with a fixed number of seats has no seat left for someone from outside. So a step
    that lets someone in has to seat two people in one chair. That is the whole proof.
    The pigeonhole does the work.


Theorem 9.1 (A permanent flip merges states, permanent_flip_is_not_inject
ive). Take any state type with a duplicate-free list of every state, any instruction type, any
step function, and any certification reading. Suppose certification is permanent: if cert(s) then
cert(step(s, i )) for every s and i. If cert(s) is false and cert(step(s, i )) is true, then the step
for i is not injective: two different states land on the same state.

Proof. Suppose the step for i were injective. Let C be the list of certified states. By
permanence the step sends C into C. Being injective, it sends C to a duplicate-free
list of the same length inside C, so it hits every member of C. The certified state
step(s, i ) is then step(c, i ) for some certified c. Injectivity gives s = c, so s is certified.
It isn’t.


Theorem 9.2 (A2 from the merging price, a2_from_merging_price_and_perma
nence). Under the same finiteness and permanence, suppose every instruction whose step
is not injective costs at least 1. Then every step that flips certification from false to true costs
at least 1. That is A2. certification_system_from_merging_price packages the
machine as a CertificationSystem with its A2 field discharged by this theorem, and p
ermanent_certification_trace_floor carries it to every trace through univer
sal_nfi_any_substrate.

Proof. The previous theorem says the flipping step is not injective. The premise prices
it at 1 or more.

There is a counting version too. permanent_flips_collapse_at_least takes k
distinct uncertified states that one instruction switches on. The step sends the certified
states and those k newcomers into the certified states, so it has at least k fewer distinct
images than inputs. Switching on more states collapses more states.

Each premise is needed, and the file proves a counterexample for each. Keep the whole
history, and every step is injective while the certificate still switches on (unbounded
_history_escapes). That is Bennett’s escape, the same move as the history lift in
Section 19, and it needs memory that grows without bound. Finite memory closes it.
Let the certificate be revoked, and flipping one bit certifies for free, then un-certifies
(revocable_certificate_escapes). Leave merging steps unpriced, and a reset
certifies at cost zero (free_merge_escapes).

This also settles the rival from Section 8. The trusted erasure system there certifies at
cost zero with no erasure reported. Its one step sends both states to certified. That
is a merge, and its erasure flag doesn’t report it (commit_without_erasure_sys


                                                 73
The Thiele Machine                                                   Devon Thiele • September 2026


tem_is_not_honest). Call erasure accounting honest when the flag reports every
instruction that merges states. Then on a finite machine with a permanent certificate,
honest erasure accounting charges the certification flip at least 1 (honest_erasure_
accounting_implies_a2). The two laws send the same bill.

What the theorem covers, and what it doesn’t. The pricing premise is Landauer’s
principle stated on the logic, a named premise. It isn’t a theorem about heat. The
51-opcode VM is not an instance, and that is by design. Its ledger is an unbounded
natural, so its state space is infinite. Every certification flip is a priced merge, while
JUMP 0 0 is a zero-cost merge, so the schedule does not price every merge. Section 9.6
proves those two facts and builds a finite piece of the VM that is an instance; it does
not classify every other merge. The theorem is about a permanent certificate on
finite hardware. The core is a pigeonhole argument, and the physics it leans on is
Landauer’s and Bennett’s (“Notes on Landauer’s principle, reversible computation,
and Maxwell’s Demon”, 2003). None of those pieces is new. CERTIFY sets a flag to
true whatever it held, and that is Landauer’s own example of an irreversible step.
That finite memory eventually has to erase is Bennett’s point. What the theorem adds
is the link, stated for every implementation at once: however a certificate that lasts
is encoded, on finite memory the step that switches it on merges states. No clever
encoding of a permanent certificate dodges that. Finiteness and permanence together
close Bennett’s history escape for the one step that certifies.

9.2 How much a permanent certificate costs
A2 says the flip costs at least one. Landauer’s principle says more: squeezing states
together costs one unit per halving. Put that in as the premise and the bill gets a size.

The shape is the same closed room. Say m states are already certified, and one step
lets k more in. All m + k of them land in the room’s m seats. Squeezing m + k states
into m places is a squeeze by a factor of (m + k )/m, and each unit of cost pays for one
                                                
halving. So the cost is at least log2 (m + k )/m .


Theorem 9.3 (The cost of switching k states on, permanent_flips_compression
_bound, permanent_flips_log_bound). Take a finite state space with a permanent
certificate, and suppose every instruction satisfies the counting premise compression_pr
iced: an instruction with cost c maps any duplicate-free list of n states onto at least n/2c
distinct states. If m states are certified and one instruction switches k distinct uncertified states
on, then
               m + k ≤ 2cost · m,           so      log2↑ (m + k ) ≤ cost + log2↑ m.

With k = 1 the cost is at least 1, which is A2 again (a2_from_compression_price_a
nd_permanence).

Proof. The k newcomers and the m certified states are m + k distinct states. Permanence
keeps the certified ones certified, and the newcomers land certified by hypothesis, so


                                                 74
The Thiele Machine                                               Devon Thiele • September 2026


all m + k land among the m certified states. The counting premise says m + k ≤ 2cost
times the number of distinct landing places, and that number is at most m. For the
logarithm form, log2↑ never decreases, and log2↑ (m · 2c ) = c + log2↑ m.

The bound is tight. Take four sheets, three blank and one stamped, and one instruction
that stamps whatever it is handed. Switching the three blanks on squeezes four states
into one, and the bound says the instruction costs at least 2 (quad_stamp_needs_tw
o). Cost 2 meets the counting premise (quad_stamp_cost_two_is_priced). That
is log2 4, paid exactly.

This is the number I wanted. A2’s “at least one” is the floor for a single flip. The real
price depends on how many states get squeezed into how few seats, and it grows
with the logarithm of the squeeze, the way Landauer’s does. The units are halvings,
not joules. Turning halvings into heat is k B T ln 2 per halving, and that conversion is
physics.

9.3 Which flips a finite machine must pay for
Step 5 asks which events every account has to price. The permanent-certificate theo-
rem suggested an answer: exactly the ones that write a record nothing takes back. I
tried to prove that. It’s false, and the true statement is sharper.

Call an instruction forced to be priced when every cost assignment that prices merges,
as Landauer’s principle does, charges it at least one. Three facts hold on any finite
machine, for any yes/no reading.


Theorem 9.4 (Forced price is merging, forced_priced_iff_merges). An instruc-
tion is forced to be priced exactly when its step merges two states.

Proof. If it merges, every merge-pricing assignment charges it. If it doesn’t, charge it
zero and charge everything else one; that assignment prices every merge and leaves
this instruction free. Building that assignment needs decidable equality on instructions.



Theorem 9.5 (Merge or revoke, flip_merges_or_revokes). If an instruction
switches the reading on somewhere, then either it merges two states, or it switches the reading
off somewhere else.

Proof. Search the certified states for one the instruction switches off. The search is finite,
so it ends. If it finds one, that’s the revocation. If it finds none, the instruction keeps the
reading on everywhere it was on, and the permanent-flip argument, applied to this one
instruction (permanent_at_flip_is_not_injective), says it merges.


Corollary 9.6 (Permanent records are forced, permanent_record_write_is_f


                                              75
The Thiele Machine                                               Devon Thiele • September 2026


orced_priced). An instruction that never switches the reading off, and switches it on
somewhere, is forced to be priced.


The converse is false. Take three states, two unmarked and one marked, and one
instruction that marks the first, unmarks the marked one, and sends the third to the
marked one. It merges the first and third, so it is forced to be priced. It also revokes a
mark, so it writes no permanent record (forced_price_without_permanent_re
cord). So “the forced events are exactly the permanent records” doesn’t hold.

Here is what does. The events a finite machine must price are the merges. Every step
that writes a permanent record is one of them. And a flip escapes the price only one
way: the same instruction takes the reading back somewhere else (injective_fl
ip_revokes). A record that stays has to be paid for. A record that’s free is one the
instruction can also erase.

That answers step 5 in a narrower form than I conjectured, and it proves something
about step 4. Step 4 says the axis belongs in the step. For an account of a finite machine
that prices merges, it isn’t optional: that account already charges every step that writes
a permanent record at least one, and at least the logarithm of the squeeze when it pays
one unit per halving (compression_priced). What no theorem here can settle
is whether every account of computation has to price merges. That’s Landauer’s
principle, and it’s physics.

9.4 The exact price can’t be read off the shadow
Step 4 says the account should carry what was established as part of how it steps. Here
is a way to test that. Say an account wants to price certification exactly: at least one for
every step that turns the reading on, and nothing for any step that doesn’t. exact_co
mmitment_pricing_characterization says that for a system whose charge is a
local predicate, every trace’s total cost meets the flip count and never exceeds it exactly
when the charged predicate is the certification flip and every charged step costs one.
The question here is who can compute it.

A shadow account sees the window before a step and the window after it. Its price
for the step is a function of those two pictures. Now put two steps side by side. They
start at the same picture and end at the same picture. One of them certified. The other
didn’t. The shadow account has one number for that pair of pictures. If the number is
at least one, it bills the step that didn’t certify. If it is zero, it misses the step that did.

   Plain reading. A clerk who sees only the before and after photos has to give two
   jobs with the same photos the same bill. If one job earned a certificate and the
   other didn’t, the clerk gets one of them wrong.


Theorem 9.7 (No exact shadow price, shadow_cannot_price_exactly). Take any
states, any instructions, any step function, any yes/no reading, and any window. Suppose two


                                              76
The Thiele Machine                                                 Devon Thiele • September 2026


steps show the same window before and the same window after, and one switches the reading
on while the other doesn’t. Then no price that is a function of the observed before and after both
meets the floor and never overcharges. Any such price that meets the floor bills a step that did
not certify (shadow_floor_overcharges).

Proof. The two steps get the same price. The floor makes it at least one. The step that
didn’t certify pays it.


Theorem 9.8 (A window that shows the reading prices exactly, window_showing_
 reading_prices_exactly). If the reading is a function of the window, then the price
“one when the window’s reading goes from off to on, zero otherwise” meets the floor and never
 overcharges. Such a window has no collision (window_showing_reading_has_no_co
 llision).


The VM has the collision for both windows I name. From the clean start, CERTIFY 0
and JUMP 1 0 leave the same program counter, registers, and memory. One certifies.
The other doesn’t. So no account that prices from bare_observable prices VM
certification exactly (vm_bare_observable_collision, vm_bare_shadow_can
not_price_exactly). Put the ledger in the window and use JUMP 1 1 instead.
Both steps then raise µ by one, and the four-field forget window has the collision
too (vm_forget_collision, vm_forget_shadow_cannot_price_exactly).

The mathematics here is one line, and it isn’t new. It is the fiber-constancy fact of
Section 3.7 (decoding_requires_fiber_constancy) with a price in place of a
decoder: nothing computed from a window can tell apart two things the window
shows the same. What the theorem adds is where it lands. The VM has such a pair, so
the exact price of certification is one of the things the usual window cannot compute.

So the exact price lives in the step, or in a window that already shows certification.
The read/write account can’t carry it. That is the part of step 4 the proofs reach. It
doesn’t say every account has to price certification exactly. It says an account that does
has to carry the reading.

9.5 The same bound in bits
The squeeze bound counts states. Landauer’s principle is usually stated in entropy.
So here is the same result in Shannon entropy.

The shape doesn’t change. Put a probability on each state that is certified or about to
flip. After the step, all of that probability sits on the m certified states. A distribution
on m states carries at most log2 m bits. So whatever the distribution had before, the
step leaves it at most log2 m.


Theorem 9.9 (Entropy ceiling after a permanent step, permanent_step_entro
py_ceiling, permanent_step_entropy_drop). Take a finite state space with a

                                               77
The Thiele Machine                                                Devon Thiele • September 2026


permanent certificate and an instruction that switches k ≥ 1 distinct uncertified states on. Let
p be any probability distribution on the states that lives on the m certified states and those k.
Then the distribution after the step has Shannon entropy at most log2 m, so the step lowers the
entropy by at least H ( p) − log2 m.

Proof. The step sends every state in play to a certified one: the certified ones by perma-
nence, the k by hypothesis. So the pushed distribution lives on the m certified states.
Entropy is at most the logarithm of the size of any set that holds the support (ent
ropy_le_log_support). That last step uses ln y ≤ y − 1 and nothing else. It is
Shannon’s bound, proved again here so the chain is checked end to end.


Corollary 9.10 (Bits removed by a permanent flip, permanent_flip_uniform_en
tropy_drop). For the uniform distribution on the m + k states in play, the step removes at
                      
least log2 (m + k )/m bits.


That is the number the counting bound gives, now in bits. The entropy form gives
a third route to A2. Call a whole-unit price entropy-priced when every instruction
costs at least the bits it removes from any distribution. On a finite machine with a
permanent certificate, an entropy-priced cost satisfies A2 (a2_from_entropy_pri
ce_and_permanence), and the trace floor follows through universal_nfi_any
_substrate (entropy_priced_trace_floor). The other side holds too. A step
that forgets nothing removes no bits from any distribution (step_entropy_invari
ant_if_injective). That is Bennett’s side, and an entropy price can leave such a
step free.

The heat is where the physics comes in. Landauer’s principle, written as a named
premise landauer_heat, says a step that lowers the entropy of the state by ∆H bits
dissipates at least k B T ln 2 · ∆H. Under that premise, a permanent flip of k states beside
m certified ones, from the uniform distribution on those m + k, dissipates at least

                                                   m+k
                                        k B T ln
                                                    m
(permanent_flip_heat_floor), and that is strictly positive when at least one
state flips and the temperature is positive (permanent_flip_heat_positive).
The premise is Landauer’s. The theorem is the step from his premise to the certificate.
It says nothing about a device until somebody measures one.

The uniform distribution is one choice, and the result doesn’t depend on it. What
Landauer’s principle charges for is the entropy a step removes from the distribution
the machine actually has. That is the generalized form of the principle (Maroney,
“Generalizing Landauer’s principle”, 2009; Sagawa, “Thermodynamic and logical
reversibilities revisited”, 2014). So the file also gives a formula for what a step removes,
and a condition under which it removes some.


                                               78
The Thiele Machine                                                 Devon Thiele • September 2026


Theorem 9.11 (When a step removes entropy, entropy_drop_as_point_sum, e
ntropy_drop_nonneg, entropy_drop_pos_of_support_merge). For any de-
terministic step and any distribution p, the entropy removed is a sum of one term per state,
                                      
p( x ) log2 q(step( x )) − log2 p( x ) over the states with p( x ) > 0, where q is the distribution
after the step. Every term is nonnegative. The sum is positive whenever two different states of
positive probability land on the same state.


Corollary 9.12 (Any spread-out distribution, permanent_flip_full_support_
entropy_drop_positive, permanent_flip_full_support_heat_positiv
e). On a finite machine with a permanent certificate, take a step that switches a state on and
any distribution that gives every certified state and the flipping state positive probability. The
step removes entropy, and under landauer_heat with positive temperature it dissipates
positive heat.


The other direction is the honest boundary. If the machine is known to be in one state
before the step, the step removes no entropy, whatever it merges, and Landauer’s
principle forces no heat at all: the premise holds exactly when heat ≥ 0 (known_st
ate_step_removes_no_entropy, known_state_flip_forces_no_heat). So
the heat a permanent certificate costs comes from uncertainty about which state the
machine is in. For a machine that already knows its state, the premise sets no heat
floor on certifying. The theorem says nothing about an actual device. The ledger still
charges it, because A2 is a rule about the step, and the step doesn’t know what the
observer knows.

9.6 A finite VM that pays for its merges
The 51-opcode VM isn’t an instance of the permanent-certificate theorem, and I don’t
want it to be. Here is what is proved instead. Every certification flip is a priced merge,
and at least one other merge is free.


Theorem 9.13 (The VM prices the certifying merge, vm_certify_merges, vm_cer
tifying_step_is_priced_merge). CERTIFY merges states: a certified state and an
uncertified state that agree on everything else land on the same result. Every VM step that
switches the certification flag on is such a merge, and it costs at least one.


Theorem 9.14 (And does not price every merge, vm_jump_is_free_merge, vm_p
rices_certifying_merge_leaves_others_free). JUMP 0 0 merges program
counters and costs zero. So the full VM does not price every merge, and the merging-price
premise fails for it.


That second theorem isn’t a gap in the VM. Pricing every merge would charge every
overwrite: every LOAD_IMM, every ADD, every STORE. That is what Landauer’s prin-
ciple asks of a physical machine, and whether every account of computation has to

                                                79
The Thiele Machine                                                 Devon Thiele • September 2026


answer to it is the physics question this book leaves open. Classical opcodes may carry
programmer-selected costs, and several structural instruction families have manda-
tory positive terms; certification is not the schedule’s only priced event. A2 holds for
the VM as a theorem. What the finite argument adds is that the certification price isn’t
arbitrary: on hardware that does price its merges, the same bill comes due. So I built a
finite machine that prices all its merges and showed the VM runs it.

The finite machine has four program slots and a certification flag: eight states. It has
three instructions. FCertify sets the flag and moves to the next slot. FJump a moves
to slot a. FNext moves to the next slot and wraps around. Their prices are 1, 2, and
0. Each price is what its squeeze demands. FCertify merges each slot’s flagged and
unflagged states, a two-to-one squeeze. FJump merges four slots into one, a four-to-one
squeeze, so it pays two halvings. FNext is a bijection and pays nothing.


Theorem 9.15 (The finite machine is an instance, fin_finite, fin_permanent, f
in_merging_priced, fin_compression_priced). The eight states are a duplicate-
free list of every state. The flag is permanent. Every merging instruction costs at least one (fc
ertify_merges, fjump_merges, fnext_injective). And every instruction meets
the counting premise: cost c squeezes any duplicate-free list at most 2c -fold. So A2 holds for it
as a theorem, twice over (fin_a2_from_merging_price, fin_a2_from_compres
sion_price).


The counting premise needed one general fact. If no image of a map has more than
K preimages in a duplicate-free list, the list has at most K times as many members as
images (fiber_bound_compression).


Theorem 9.16 (The VM runs it, vm_runs_finite_machine, vm_pays_finite_pr
ice, vm_runs_finite_trace). Read a VM state through the window (program counter
modulo four, certification flag). Then CERTIFY 0, JUMP a 2, and CHECKPOINT "" 0
step exactly as FCertify, FJump a, and FNext do, and the VM ledger rises by exactly the
finite machine’s price. The same holds for whole runs.


Corollary 9.17 (vm_fragment_certification_paid). Any VM run built from
those three instructions that starts uncertified and ends certified has raised µ by at least one.
That payment comes from the finite machine’s derived price. No pricing rule is supplied for the
purpose.


Two limits. The price of two on JUMP is the program’s choice of mu_delta. The
VM’s schedule allows it and doesn’t force it. The theorem proves that certification
flips are priced and exhibits one free non-certifying merge; it does not classify every
other merge. And the window is a projection. The VM’s full state keeps more than
the eight finite states do, its ledger included. What the theorem shows is that the


                                               80
The Thiele Machine                                                      Devon Thiele • September 2026


permanent-certificate argument isn’t about an imaginary machine. A fragment of the
VM I built runs it, and pays what Landauer’s principle would ask of it.

9.7 Learning is free. Committing is not.
Everything so far prices one event: the step that switches a certificate on. The obvious
next question is whether narrowing things down costs too. Every time a computation
rules out a possibility, it knows more. Does knowing more cost?

It depends on whose possibilities they are.

Start with the machine’s own. Say the machine could be in any state of some set, and
it runs a fixed program. Afterwards it could be in fewer states, because some of them
landed together. Every time two states land together, that is a merge, and merges are
what the price charges. So the machine’s own spread can’t shrink for free.

An observer is different. The observer watches a window and crosses off the starting
states that don’t match what it saw. That list can shrink while the machine forgets
nothing at all. Picture a hidden bit and a blank display. One step adds the bit into
the display. No two states land together, so merge pricing does not force a positive
charge. The file exhibits one admissible cost assigning that injective step zero; the
lower-bound pricing rule would also permit overcharging it. The machine can still
be in either of its two states. But the observer, reading the display, now knows which
one.

   Plain reading. The machine can’t squeeze its own possibilities for free. An observer
   can learn for free, because learning doesn’t have to squeeze anything. The bill
   comes later, when the display has to be wiped for the next reading. Wiping is a
   merge.


Theorem 9.18 (The machine’s narrowing is priced, run_narrowing_priced_log).
Take a finite deterministic machine whose costs meet the squeeze price compression_pric
ed. For any duplicate-free list D of starting states and any program t, let D ′ be the states the
machine can be in after running t from somewhere in D. Then

                               log2↑ | D | ≤ cost(t) + log2↑ | D ′ |.

The run itself is the witness. No decision tree, payment proof, or representative map is supplied.

Proof. Induction on the program. One instruction with cost c squeezes a duplicate-
free list by at most 2c , and the states reachable after the rest of the program from the
squeezed list are exactly the states reachable after the whole program from the original
list. Multiply the squeezes.


Theorem 9.19 (An observer’s narrowing can be free, observer_narrowing_can


                                                81
The Thiele Machine                                               Devon Thiele • September 2026


_be_free). There is a finite machine, with prices that meet the squeeze price, and a one-
instruction program of cost zero, such that an observer who watches the display along the run
goes from two candidate starting states to one. So the observer reading of the target,

                                log2↑ |Ω| − log2↑ |Ω′ | ≤ ∆µ,

with Ω′ the starting states consistent with what the observer saw, fails for that machine under
that one merge-priced cost assignment. Another admissible cost may charge the injective step.

Proof. The states are pairs (hidden bit, display). Measure adds the bit into the display
by exclusive-or and costs zero. It is a bijection on the four states, so it meets the
squeeze price. Start with the display blank. Before the step both starting states show a
blank display. After it, the display equals the bit. The observer’s list drops from two
to one; the cost is zero. The machine’s own spread stays at two (demon_machine_sp
read_kept).


Theorem 9.20 (Wiping the record is priced, wipe_costs_at_least_one). In the
same machine, Wipe blanks the display. Every cost that meets the squeeze price charges it at
least one.


The VM does the same thing. XOR_ADD 2 1 0 adds register 1 into a blank register 2
at zero cost. An observer watching register 2 goes from two candidate states to one,
and the ledger stays at zero (vm_observer_narrowing_at_zero_cost).

What the run itself teaches is the fairer measure. The observer’s first look already
crosses off the starting states whose window differs, before any step runs. So compare
what it knows after that first look with what it knows at the end. Under the same
cost assignment the measurement is still free (demon_refutes_incremental). The
smallest machine that teaches for free has three states (free_incremental_narr
owing_with_three). Cycle through them, seen as false, false, true; the two states
that look alike at the start come apart after one step, and a cycle forgets nothing. Two
states are not enough (no_free_incremental_narrowing_below_three). Two
starting states that look alike at the start are then every state there is, so the window
shows the same thing everywhere and never teaches anything.

None of this is new physics. It is Bennett’s answer to Maxwell’s demon (“The thermo-
dynamics of computation, a review”, 1982), stated on the logic and checked in Coq.
Measuring can be done without merging. What costs is making room again.

It also says exactly which insight No Free Insight prices. Raw learning can be free,
the way Tier 0 of Section 6 already is. The insight that can’t be free is certified in-
sight: committing to what you learned, in a record the machine can’t take back. The
structural-entitlement bound of Section 5 still holds, and it holds because it is handed
a trace that paid for its decision tree. This section shows that payment is a premise.


                                              82
The Thiele Machine                                            Devon Thiele • September 2026


The narrowing alone doesn’t force it.

10 The substrate and its halting-shaped wall
The Thiele Machine is not the 51 opcodes. The 51 opcodes are scaffolding. They
make the substrate concrete enough to verify in Coq, run as OCaml, and synthesize to
silicon, but they are not the substrate itself. The substrate is the step relation together
with the cost-ledger monotonicity rule: every atomic transition either preserves µ or
charges it. PNEW, MORPH, CHSH_LASSERT, the whole instruction set. Those are
the experimental apparatus that gives the substrate a concrete realization to probe.
Strip the opcodes and the substrate is still there. Add different opcodes that respect
the substrate’s monotonicity discipline and the substrate is still the same substrate.

The Turing machine has the same shape. Tape, head, and transition table are scaffold-
ing for one realization. Register machines, lambda calculus, counter machines, RAMs,
and partial recursive functions all share the same step-relation shape with different
scaffolding (only nat_substrate and the VM are Substrate instances here); the
equivalences between them are theorems, and that this class captures effective com-
putation at all is the Church-Turing thesis. They are all the Turing machine in the
sense that matters. Their primitive steps and cost models can differ even when their
computable functions agree.

The result that follows is a fact about the substrate, not about the 51 opcodes.
The opcodes are one realization. Other substrates that supply Substrate and
WithShortcutPredicate inherit the same theorem, for deciders whose flip trans-
former the substrate can represent as one of its own programs.

Relationship to Rice’s theorem. The result that follows is Kleene’s 1938 diagonal pat-
tern instantiated at the substrate level. Rice’s 1953 theorem applies the same diagonal
to non-trivial extensional properties of recursive functions computed by Turing ma-
chines; the substrate-level version below applies the very same diagonal to non-trivial
extensional predicates of substrate programs in any model that exposes an internal
recursion theorem and an internal representability predicate. It is the same trick, two
floors apart, which is the whole point. The formal content is the diagonal construc-
tion; what the substrate-level framing adds is the parameterization (any substrate
satisfying the typeclass interface, not just the standard Turing model) and the choice
of extensional predicate (AdmitsShortcut, a supplied predicate: on the VM, equal-
ity of the bounded run with that of simple_morph_trace; on nat_substrate d,
nat_run d p 0 = Some 1). The result is therefore a substrate-level instance of the
pattern Rice used; the result does not fall out as a corollary of Rice’s theorem in its
original setting, because Rice’s premises are about partial recursive functions in the
Turing-machine model, and nothing in them knows about arbitrary substrates car-
rying their own recursion theorems and representability predicates. The formalized
result should be read with its own interface premises. Its proof structure does not


                                            83
The Thiele Machine                                          Devon Thiele • September 2026


establish a non-reducibility or Turing-degree separation from classical halting.

10.1 The Substrate typeclass
A Coq typeclass is a checklist of operations and properties a type has to supply to
count as the thing. The Substrate interface used here packages the following data
and premises independently of any particular instruction set. It does not itself contain
a certification predicate or A2:

   • A type state of substrate states.
   • A type Program of substrate programs.
   • An outcome function run : Program → state → option state. The
     record does not impose a correspondence between this function and step, or a
     divergence interpretation of None; those require an instance contract. The VM
     instance always returns Some of its 1000-fuel result, including when that result
     has not halted.
   • A cost function mu :     state → nat.
   • An atomic step relation step :      Program → state → state → Prop.
   • A cost-ledger monotonicity field Substrate.mu_monotone: forall p s
     s’, step p s s’ → mu s ≤ mu s’. This is a separate general-purpose
     monotonicity property from the kernel’s A2 (which specifically asserts that a
     step flipping certification false-to-true costs ≥ 1, and which is carried by the C
     ertificationSystem record, not by this typeclass). Both properties hold in
     the VM: all instruction costs are non-negative and the cert transition costs ≥ 1.
     See Section A.8 for the A2 statement and the related axioms A3 through A6.
   • A Gödel coding:           functions encode : Program → state and
     decode_safe : state → Program with the round-trip property
     decode_safe (encode p) = p. This states recovery by the supplied
     mathematical decoder; it does not assert that a program in the substrate can
     execute that decoder or inspect the encoded field.
   • An internal-representability predicate Representable : (Program →
     Program) → Prop, scoping which Coq function transformers the substrate
     can express as programs of its own kind. Concrete substrates instantiate this with
     a precise meaning; the minimal nat-coded substrate defines Representable
     f as “f is the diagonal flip transformer for the substrate’s own decide function,”
     which is exactly the shape the diagonalization needs.
   • A recursion-theorem field, internal form: for every internally representable trans-
     former f : Program → Program, there is a program p extensionally equiv-
     alent to f p, where extensionally equivalent means the two give the same result
     on every input, however differently they are written.




                                          84
The Thiele Machine                                           Devon Thiele • September 2026


The recursion-theorem field is the load-bearing piece. It is the substrate’s Kleene
1938 recursion theorem, restricted to transformers the substrate can actually express.
The restriction is not a hedge; it is the substrate’s own scope. Turing 1936 did not
prove “no decider exists in any conceivable mathematical universe”; he proved “no
Turing machine decides halting,” because Turing machines were the substrate Turing
was studying. The restriction to internally representable transformers is the same
scope move at the substrate level: the substrate’s limitative theorem is about what the
substrate itself can express, not about every Coq function that exists in the meta-theory.

The recursion theorem is not derivable from mu_is_initial_monotone (the cost-
functional uniqueness result). Initiality of the cost functional states that a measure
initialized to zero and satisfying the exact increment law M (vm_apply s i) = M
s + instruction_cost i agrees with µ on reachable states. It does not select
the instruction-price schedule, identify measures on unreachable states, or collapse
all monotone measures to one inhabitant. The recursion theorem instead supplies
extensional fixed points for transformers satisfying the chosen representability predi-
cate. In the nat construction it is proved separately for each parameter d, whose flip
determines that instance’s restricted representability class and code-2 behavior.

10.2 The shortcut-admittance predicate
The substrate becomes interesting for the limitative result when it is equipped with
a non-trivial shortcut-admittance predicate: a Prop-valued predicate AdmitsShortcut
on programs, witnessed by at least one program that admits and at least one that
does not, and depending only on extensional behavior (programs with the same run
behavior on every state admit shortcuts iff each other).

The typeclass extension WithShortcutPredicate packages this:                         an
AdmitsShortcut field, two canonical witnesses yes_program and no_program
with their respective inhabitedness or non-inhabitedness lemmas, and the extensional-
ity field. These are the conditions the diagonalization needs. A substrate that supplies
them earns the limitative theorem.

The kernel constructs a concrete nat-coded family and a VM instance parameterized
by its representability class and recurrence premise.

For each d : N → bool, nat_substrate d supplies the typeclass fields for a lan-
guage with distinguished codes 0, 1, and 2. Its representability predicate selects the
flip transformer associated with d, and code 2 supplies its fixed point. The theorem
nat_self_undecidable states that d fails to decide nat_admits d. The global
assumption report is closed; d remains an explicit mathematical parameter.

The 51-opcode VM substrate vm_substrate is parameterized over the VM-specific
Gödel encoding (vm_encode, vm_decode_safe, the round-trip), the VM’s internal-
representability predicate (vm_representable), and the VM’s internal recursion
theorem restricted to representable transformers. The Coq Section discipline is iden-


                                           85
The Thiele Machine                                            Devon Thiele • September 2026


tical to the one the physical bridges use for their named constants: the parameters
are not global axioms; closing the surrounding Section discharges them as explicit
forall premises on every consumer. The encoding is a Section parameter too,
though not a live one: the kernel’s closed round-trip lemma (program_to_nat,
nat_to_program, no Hypothesis) discharges it, which is exactly what vm_stru
ctural_shortcut_undecidable_encoded does. Its conclusion rules out a de-
cider whose answer is true if and only if the program admits the shortcut, whenever
that decider’s flip is representable. The VM’s internal-representability predicate and
bounded recurrence premise remain explicit parameters of the encoded theorem. The
generic theorem is independent of this particular VM instance, but still requires its own
interface premises. The separate family nat_substrate d supplies those premises
only for its d-dependent semantics and representability class; it does not discharge the
VM premise.

The VM-side AdmitsShortcut is the extensional predicate “this VM program’s
bounded run from init_state coincides with the bounded run of simple_mor
ph_trace.” yes_program is simple_morph_trace itself; no_program is the
empty trace (which produces init_state, demonstrably distinct from simple_m
orph_final, since the latter has fired the supra-cert channel and the former has
not). The extensionality field is the standard “equal run on every state implies the
predicates agree.” The instantiation that assembles these into the WithShortcutPre
dicate interface lives in the kernel.

10.3 The substrate-level limitative theorem

   Plain reading. Kleene 1938’s diagonal, transplanted from time to structure. Same
   shape: assume a decider, build a flipper, find a fixed point, watch the program
   disagree with itself in both cases. The work is in the typeclass: once the substrate
   has its own recursion theorem, two canonical inhabitants on opposite sides of the
   predicate, and extensionality, the diagonal lands itself. What it rules out is a correct
   decider whose flip belongs to the supplied representability class. Pointing it at
   a concrete execution model takes that model’s fixed-point premise. The generic
   proof doesn’t supply it.


Theorem 10.1 (Structural-axis undecidability, substrate-level). For every Substrate-
instance equipped with a WithShortcutPredicate extension, there is no Coq-definable function
decide : Program → bool whose corresponding diagonal flip transformer is inter-
nally representable in the substrate and that for all programs p satisfies: decide p = true
iff AdmitsShortcut p.

Proof. Assume for contradiction such a decide exists. Define the diagonal flip trans-




                                            86
The Thiele Machine                                                Devon Thiele • September 2026


former                            
                                  no_program         if decide p = true
                     flip( p) =
                                  yes_program if decide p = false

By hypothesis, flip is internally representable in the substrate. Apply the sub-
strate’s typeclass field recursion_theorem to flip: there exists p such that
prog_equiv p (flip p), where prog_equiv is the extensional equivalence “run
agrees on every initial state.”

Case-split on decide p. Case A (decide p = true): by the bi-implication,
AdmitsShortcut p; by definition of flip, flip p = no_program; by the fixed-point
witness, prog_equiv p no_program; by extensionality of AdmitsShortcut (typeclass
field), AdmitsShortcut p iff AdmitsShortcut no_program. The latter is false
(typeclass field no_program_refuses). Contradiction.

Case B (decide p = false):              symmetrically, by the bi-implication ¬
AdmitsShortcut p; flip p = yes_program; prog_equiv p yes_program; ex-
tensionality yields AdmitsShortcut p iff AdmitsShortcut yes_program. The
latter is true (typeclass field yes_program_admits). Contradiction.

Both cases close. No such decide exists. PrintAssumptionsstructural_short
cut_undecidable returns Closed under the global context.

   Plain reading. For each candidate d, the construction builds an instance and proves
   that d fails on that instance’s predicate Pd . The instance itself depends on d. This
   is a family theorem. It doesn’t hand you one fixed effective predicate that beats
   every effective decider.


Theorem 10.2 (Structural-axis undecidability for the nat-coded substrate). For every
Coq function d : nat → bool, the substrate nat_substrate d cannot internally
decide its own structural-shortcut membership predicate. In particular, d itself is not a correct
decider for the predicate of the substrate it parameterizes; the corollary nat_self_undeci
dable reads off this self-referential conclusion directly.

Proof. For each fixed d, the substrate nat_substrate d supplies every typeclass
field constructively: Program := nat; yes_program := 0; no_program := 1;
nat_run d is a finite Coq pattern match using d(2); prog_equiv is pointwise equal-
ity of run; Representable f is the precise predicate “f is the diagonal flip trans-
former for the substrate’s own decide function”; recursion_theorem is discharged
by exhibiting code 2 as the fixed point of any such transformer (its run is defined to
mirror whatever the transformer outputs); AdmitsShortcut is extensional by con-
struction; yes_program_admits and no_program_refuses hold by definitional
unfolding of run at 0 and 1. Apply the substrate-level theorem above. The corollary
nat_self_undecidable specializes the conclusion to d itself: d cannot be a correct


                                               87
The Thiele Machine                                          Devon Thiele • September 2026


decider for nat_admits d, cannot answer true exactly when the predicate holds.
Print Assumptions on both nat_structural_shortcut_undecidable and
nat_self_undecidable returns Closed under the global context.

   Plain reading. The VM version holds only under the bounded recurrence and
   representability premises printed in the statement. The nat family gives a separate
   concrete example for each candidate decider. It doesn’t discharge the VM premise.
   Unbounded execution and a self-interpreter need their own semantics and proofs,
   and the next subsection has them.


Theorem 10.3 (Structural-axis undecidability for the 51-opcode VM, conditional
on the VM’s own recursion theorem). Assume the VM’s internal recursion theorem
restricted to representable transformers: for every transformer f on VM programs with
vm_representable f, there exists a program p such that vm_run p and vm_run
(f p) agree on every initial state. Under that assumption, there is no Coq function
decide : list vm_instruction → bool whose diagonal flip transformer sat-
isfies vm_representable and that for all programs p satisfies: decide p = true iff
vm_admits_shortcut_extensionalp.

Proof. Instantiate the substrate-level theorem at vm_substrate. The Gödel encoding
piece (vm_encode, vm_decode_safe, the round-trip) enters as Section parameters
and is then discharged by the kernel’s closed round-trip lemma (program_to_nat,
nat_to_program, no Hypothesis) in vm_structural_shortcut_undecidable
_encoded. The VM’s internal-representability predicate vm_representable and
the VM’s internal recursion theorem restricted to representable transformers are the
Section parameters that stay live; closing the surrounding Coq Section discharges
them as explicit forall premises on every consumer, the same Section-parameter
discipline the physical bridges use for their named constants, and the theorem above
states that recursion-theorem premise directly rather than leaving it implicit in the
proof. The VM-side AdmitsShortcut is the extensional predicate “this VM pro-
gram’s bounded run from init_state coincides with the bounded run of simpl
e_morph_trace”; yes_program is simple_morph_trace; no_program is the
empty trace, which produces init_state and so differs from simple_morph_fin
al (where the supra-cert channel has fired). The substrate-level diagonal applies; vm_
structural_shortcut_undecidable is its specialization, carrying the VM-side
parameters as premises. The nat family above is a separate application with different
semantics. This VM specialization is conditional on its stated bounded recurrence
premise and representability class.

vm_run returns Some (run_vm 1000 p s) even when fuel expires before halting.
Equality of its two finite-data results is decidable externally. Under the recurrence
premise, the flip of that correct external decider must therefore lie outside the chosen
