MORPH_GET(r, id, sel, δ) Read field (0=source, 1=target, 2=#coupling- δ
                         pairs, 3=is-identity) of morphism id into r.

MORPH_ASSERT deserves special attention. It charges S(δ) ≥ 1 regardless of whether
the assertion succeeds. This is the sharpest instance of No Free Insight: the attempt
to claim structural knowledge costs something, even when the claim is false. A failed
assertion is not free. A step rule with no ledger has nowhere to record that charge. An
ordinary machine that keeps a ledger in its own step rule can record it, and then it
is an instance of the substrate. In the 51-opcode realisation it is enforced by the step
semantics and tied back to the abstract theorem.

12.8 Why 51 opcodes?
The number 51 comes from what the kernel needs to do:

   • Compute arithmetic and data: 14 ops (LOAD_IMM, XFER, ADD, SUB, MUL, AND,
     OR, SHL, SHR, LUI, XOR_LOAD, XOR_ADD, XOR_SWAP, XOR_RANK)
   • Memory and control flow: 8 ops (LOAD, STORE, JUMP, JNEZ, CALL, RET, HALT,
     CHECKPOINT)
   • I/O, heap, and tensor: 7 ops (READ_PORT, WRITE_PORT, EMIT, HEAP_LOAD,
     HEAP_STORE, TENSOR_SET, TENSOR_GET)
   • Partition structure: 4 ops (PNEW, PSPLIT, PMERGE, PDISCOVER)
   • Logic and certification: 3 ops (LASSERT, LJOIN, MDLACC)


                                          110
The Thiele Machine                                          Devon Thiele • September 2026


   • Witness and certification (general plus the full CHSH check family): 8 ops
     (CHSH_TRIAL, CERTIFY, REVEAL, CHSH_LASSERT, CHSH_LASSERT_1AB, CH
     SH_LASSERT_1AB_G5, CHSH_LASSERT_1AB_G345, CHSH_LASSERT_1AB_G
     12345)
   • Categorical morphisms: 7 ops (MORPH, COMPOSE, MORPH_ID, MORPH_DELETE,
     MORPH_ASSERT, MORPH_TENSOR, MORPH_GET)

14 + 8 + 7 + 4 + 3 + 8 + 7 = 51. Of the 51, 47 are synthesized into the FPGA bit-
stream; the four Q1+AB check opcodes (CHSH_LASSERT_1AB and its three γ-extended
variants) live in the Kami HW abstraction with kernel-equivalence proven, but not
in the synthesized bitstream (see the “CHSH check family” note just below for the
silicon-budget reason).

The CHSH check family: CHSH_LASSERT plus four Q1+AB extensions. The base
opcode CHSH_LASSERT mu_delta reads the eight buckets of vm_witness directly,
computes the integer-arithmetic check column_contractive_check_witness
(defined using only Z arithmetic on the same/diff counts), and either advances the
program counter on success or traps to LASSERT_TRAP_PC and latches vm_err on
failure. The cost is S(mu_delta) ≥ 1 regardless of outcome. Mandatory-floor discipline:
you pay to check, even to fail. The bridge theorem chsh_lassert_no_trap_impl
ies_npa_psd proves that any no-trap, no-err-flip execution of CHSH_LASSERT from
an error-free state implies PSD of the witness-derived, fixed 5 × 5 real moment matrix.
The condition is NPA’s level-1 PSD test, and the name says only that. The kernel does
not construct a quantum state or observables. The chain in plain order: the integer
check on the witness counters passes; that fact lifts to real-valued column-contractivity
by column_contractive_check_witness_sound; column-contractivity is bicon-
ditional with PSD of the specified matrix by column_contractive_iff_npa_ps
d. Three theorems. No physics input. The opcode is the place in the kernel where
the algebra of Section 18 becomes something the VM actually refuses to do, not just
declines to recommend.

The four Q1+AB extensions CHSH_LASSERT_1AB (tag 0x2F, γ = 0), CHSH_LASSERT
_1AB_G5 (tag 0x30), CHSH_LASSERT_1AB_G345 (tag 0x31), and CHSH_LASSERT_
1AB_G12345 (tag 0x32) lift the same discipline from the level-1 5 × 5 matrix to the
level-1+AB 9 × 9 formal matrix. Each runs its own integer-arithmetic Schur-cascade
check on vm_witness plus the supplied γ-bucket pairs, advances on pass or traps on
fail, and charges S(mu_delta) ≥ 1. From an error-free state, the four bridge theorems
(one per slice, named chsh_lassert_1ab_*_no_trap_implies_npa_psd_q1
ab) deliver npa_psd_q1ab = symmetric9 ∧ PSD9 on the witness-derived rationals.
The predicate is PSD of that formal matrix. It is not a hardware or Hilbert-space
construction. The mathematical detail of the four-slice Schur cascade is in §18’s Q1+AB
subsection below.



                                          111
The Thiele Machine                                           Devon Thiele • September 2026


The instruction set separates ordinary register and memory operations from graph
operations, witness recording, and designated charged checks. CERTIFY activates
vm_certified; MORPH constructs morphism relationships; CHSH_TRIAL records
binary-game outcomes; and the CHSH assertion family tests the corresponding arith-
metic constraints. Their costs and effects are specified in VMStep.v.

13 What a categorical morphism is: the category theory, from scratch
Category theory from scratch. I didn’t know what it was when I started this project.

This is the structure layer of the VM build. It is the kind of structure the VM happens
to carry. The model in Part I doesn’t ask for it, and none of the abstract theorems there
mention it.

13.1 The idea of a category
A category is the part of mathematics that takes the relationships between things as
seriously as the things, and then quietly decides the relationships matter more. It
is built from two ingredients. There are objects, the things. And there are arrows
between them, the relationships. That is the entire inventory, and it looks thin until
you notice what got promoted. In most of mathematics the objects are the citizens and
the maps between them are second-class traffic. A category turns that around. The
arrows are first-class, and nearly everything you can say inside a category you say by
pointing at arrows, not by reaching inside objects.

The objects can be anything at all, and that is at once why category theory reaches as
far as it does and why it feels slippery the first time through. Sets can be the objects.
So can vector spaces, or topological spaces, or any kind of mathematical thing that has
structure-respecting maps between its members. The theory does not care what the
objects are made of. It cares only how they connect. In the realisation here the objects
are modules, the named pieces a partition cut the state into, and the arrows are the
relationships between those pieces.

Arrows go by a second name, morphisms, and the two words are interchangeable;
I use both. An arrow runs from one object to another, written f : A → B and read
“ f , from A to B.” The thing everyone imports from school and has to set down here
is the assumption that an arrow is a function. It does not have to be. An arrow is
whatever the category accepts as a structure-respecting way to get from A to B, and
in the realisation here it is something more pointed than a function. A morphism is
a claim: the assertion that these two modules are related, and here is the type of the
relationship.

Two laws must hold:

   1. Composition: If f : A → B and g : B → C, there must exist a composite
      g ◦ f : A → C. This is what COMPOSE implements.


                                          112
The Thiele Machine                                             Devon Thiele • September 2026


   2. Identity: For every object A, there must exist an identity arrow id A : A → A.
      This is what MORPH_ID implements.

And composition must be associative: (h ◦ g) ◦ f = h ◦ ( g ◦ f ).

                         id A


                                     f                   g
                          A                    B                    C


                                             g◦ f


Figure 5: Arrows chain. f goes from A to B, g goes from B to C, and composing them
gives one arrow straight from A to C. Every object also has an identity arrow looping
back to itself. COMPOSE builds the long arrow; MORPH_ID builds the loop.


Why composition? Because relationships have to chain. A to B, B to C, therefore A to
C. Without that, every multi-hop path has to be named individually, and the structure
collapses into a pile of unconnected pairs. A category without composition is a phone
book without a search function.

Why identity? Every object needs a self-loop, even the boring ones. A module with no
connections to anything else still has to exist somewhere in the framework. MORPH_ID
is the instruction for that: it builds an A → A morphism, stores an empty coupling,
and flags it is_identity. That flag is load-bearing, and here is why it has to be.
An empty coupling would annihilate under relational composition (compose anything
with the empty relation and you get the empty relation back, not the thing you started
with), so the identity cannot be carried by the coupling data. It is carried by the
flag, which COMPOSE honors: composing an identity-flagged arrow with f returns f ’s
coupling unchanged, so id A ; f = f and f ; idB = f hold at the coupling level (grap
h_compose_morphisms_coupling; the matching hardware path is driven_ste
p_compose). Drop the flag and the identity law breaks: composing with an identity
would wipe out the very thing you composed it with.

Why associativity? Parenthesization should not matter. (h ◦ g) ◦ f and h ◦ ( g ◦ f ) both
spell the same three-hop path A → B → C → D. If they came out different, two
programs that built the same chain in different orders would produce two different
morphisms. That is a bug. The chain is the thing. How you assembled it is not.

Here is exactly what Coq checks, on the stored morphism records. Morphisms carry
coupling data, a list of (source-cell, target-cell) pairs plus a label. A “cell” is a natural
number indexing into a module’s memory region. A coupling pair ( a, c) says: cell
a in the source module is related to cell c in the target module. The coupling is the
relational content of the morphism, the actual evidence of which parts of one module

                                            113
The Thiele Machine                                            Devon Thiele • September 2026


correspond to which parts of another. COMPOSE produces a new morphism whose
coupling pairs are the relational composition of the two inputs: ( a, c) is included when
there exists an intermediate cell b such that ( a, b) is in the first coupling and (b, c) is
in the second. Standard relational composition. The one exception is the identity
short-circuit from the previous subsection: if either input is flagged is_identity,
COMPOSE returns the other input’s coupling unchanged. graph_compose_morphi
sms_coupling proves both cases. I checked that in Coq, because the identity law
holding “obviously” is exactly the kind of thing my eye waves through right before
it turns out to be false. Associativity is morph_graph_compose_assoc: for three
looked-up morphisms whose endpoints match, the two groupings of the relational
composition of their coupling lists give the same set of pairs. Same set, not necessarily
the same list order. It is stated on the coupling lists, not on COMPOSE with its identity-
flag branch. Calling the whole thing a full category takes more: explicit membership
for objects and for arrows between them, and equality laws. IDs, labels, and list order
don’t supply those on their own. The identity flag is how the implementation handles
identities. By itself it doesn’t prove that every isolated object sits in a fully specified
category.

13.2 Why modules as objects
In the concrete kernel, the objects of the category are the modules of the partition
graph, and that choice is less arbitrary than it looks. A module is a named piece of
the computation, the place where a partition cut the state and put a fence around it.
Drawing that fence is the act that creates an address. Once a piece has a name it owns a
region of memory or state, it may carry certified axioms, it carries a local 4×4 µ-tensor,
and, the part that earns it the job, it becomes a thing the rest of the structure can point
at. That is the whole reason modules are the bookkeeping unit: a relationship has
to attach to something, and the only somethings on offer are the named pieces. An
unnamed piece, as far as the category is concerned, is not there at all. There is nothing
to relate it to and nothing to relate it from. So everything structural hangs off a module.
That isn’t a convention. A module is the only kind of thing the category can see.

A morphism between two modules is a claim with its evidence stapled on: “there is
a named relationship between A and B, and here is the explicit coupling data that
says which parts line up.” That is the move that turns the relationship into a first-
class object instead of a remark in a comment somewhere. COMPOSE chains two of
these by the relational composition already spelled out above, and an identity-flagged
input still falls to the short-circuit from before; I will not re-derive either one here.
What is worth stopping on is that the relationship does not dissolve into the modules
it connects once it is built. It stays in the graph as a thing you can hold and ask
questions of: via MORPH_GET (selector 2) you read back the coupling-pair count of any
morphism, after the fact, straight off the state. The relationship is not lost in the act of
relating. It is its own object, carrying a count you can go and demand back.

What the coupling pairs represent is up to the programmer. They could encode:

                                            114
The Thiele Machine                                           Devon Thiele • September 2026


   • Module B refines module A (which cells in A map to which cells in B)
   • Module A entangles with module B (which cell-pairs are correlated)
   • Module A simulates module B (which transitions/outputs correspond)

The kernel enforces the relational composition correctly. The interpretation is yours.

13.3 Why this is not just “extra state”
Here is the question I chewed on for a long time. If you can just bolt extra registers
onto a classical machine and stash morphism IDs in them, why does the categorical
layer matter at all?

The answer is in the MORPH_ASSERT opcode and in the Categorical Separation Theo-
rem.

Extra registers do not have provenance. You can write anything into a register. You
can write 42 into register 5 and claim it is a morphism ID. But the VM checks: does
morphism 42 actually exist in the graph, put there by a MORPH or COMPOSE instruction
that genuinely ran? MORPH_ASSERT looks the morphism up by ID and fails (setting
vm_err) if no such morphism is there. Existence is all it verifies; the source and target
are not arguments it checks against, though you can read them back separately with
MORPH_GET (selectors 0 and 1). And MORPH_ASSERT costs S(δ) ≥ 1 even if it fails.

Building the morphism chain by calling MORPH, COMPOSE, and MORPH_ID is free when
those instructions carry δ = 0. Those ops don’t have a mandatory positive floor. What
costs is MORPH_ASSERT: the act of asserting that the morphism exists and activating
the cert-address channel. That always costs S(δ) ≥ 1. You can build the chain for free,
but you cannot assert it into the cert-address channel for free.

The graph is structure the machine holds outright. A trusted trace records every
change to it and what each assertion attempt cost. MORPH_ASSERT checks that the
morphism exists and stores a checksum of the property string. It doesn’t check what
the property says, and it doesn’t check the certificate you hand it. The separation
theorem in the next section says exactly what the chosen shadow throws away.

14 Categorical separation: what the classical shadow drops
Here is the smallest version of the whole picture. Two states, side by side. Every
classical field matches: registers, memory, µ, program counter, error flag, certification
flag. One of them holds an identity arrow in its graph. The other holds nothing.
Through the six-field window they are the same state. They aren’t.

That is a fact about this particular window. Section 3 already pulled the transition law
apart from what an observer sees. A different representation could keep the graph.
This section proves what this window, as chosen, drops.




                                          115
The Thiele Machine                                                 Devon Thiele • September 2026


Theorem 14.1 (Categorical Separation). There exist states s1 and s2 such that:

   • s1 .vm_regs = s2 .vm_regs (identical registers)
   • s1 .vm_mem = s2 .vm_mem (identical memory)
   • s1 .vm_mu = s2 .vm_mu (identical µ)
   • s1 .vm_err = s2 .vm_err (identical error flag)
   • s1 .vm_pc = s2 .vm_pc (identical program counter)
   • s1 .vm_certified = s2 .vm_certified (identical top-level certification flag)

yet their morphism graphs differ. In the concrete witness, one state has an identity morphism
in pg_morphisms; the other has no morphisms.

Construction. Build s1 with pg_morphisms = [(0, id)].                Build s2 with
pg_morphisms = []. Set every shadow field the same: registers, memory, µ, PC,
error flag, and vm_certified. The equality proof is by reflexivity on those fields.
The graph distinction is by list constructor mismatch: a one-element morphism list is
not the empty list.


Two witness states, side by side. What the six-field shadow sees. What the graph
state actually holds.

                                               State s1    State s2
                            vm_regs            []          []
                            vm_mem             []          []
                            vm_mu              0           0
                            vm_pc              0           0
                            vm_err             false       false
                            vm_certified       false       false
                            pg_morphisms       [(0, id)]   []


What this witness shows. The two states above are not contrived. They are the
simplest possible six-field shadow collision under the Thiele projection: identical
registers, identical memory, identical PC, identical error flag, identical µ, identical
vm_certified. Every observer restricted to that projection says they are the same
state. The morphism graph says they are not. This is a witness inside the model that
the chosen projection drops information. On its own it doesn’t compare substrates: a
similar projection on any other substrate makes the same shape. What ties it to the
rest of the argument is the cost floor, the irredundancy result, and the fact that A2 can
be a law in the step instead of a check run afterward. The uniqueness theorem says
the ledger is the only count that fits its schedule. Pricing and irredundancy say why
those fields, and that law, are the ones worth keeping.



                                            116
The Thiele Machine                                             Devon Thiele • September 2026


What the witness does not prove on its own. The obvious move is to reach for
more room: bolt on a register, a tape region, any auxiliary slot, and surely the Turing
machine wriggles out. It doesn’t. The same argument, made by hand and not in Coq,
rebuilds the witness one size up. The augmented machine still has a non-identity
projection (drop the added slot from the projected fields) and the same collision shape
on the augmented states. The collapse the shadow lemma exhibits happens no matter
how much room you give it, because it is baked into the construction. That cuts both
ways. It is also why the witness, on its own, says nothing about whether a Turing
machine can hold the graph. It can, encoded on tape. The question that matters lives
somewhere else, and the next paragraph says where.

The deeper question is where the cost rule lives, and that is the substrate-versus-
program distinction. Section 3’s subsection “What is canonical, and under which
conditions” says it once and explicitly: a fixed machine can enforce an invariant
through its own transitions. That includes a Turing machine. Give its tape a ledger
track and a certification cell, and write a transition table that never sets the cell without
bumping the ledger. That machine obeys A2 in its step relation. It is an instance
of CertificationSystem, not a counterexample to it. What a Turing machine
does not do is enforce A2 on the programs it runs. A universal machine simulating
another machine carries the simulated certification bit as data on its tape, and a buggy
simulated program can flip that bit without paying; the host’s step relation has no
opinion about it. The Thiele VM is in exactly the same position when it simulates
something else. The kernel theorems are about the designated predicate of the machine
doing the stepping. That is a statement about which predicate a step rule prices. It is
not a claim that Turing machines cannot price one.

Connecting to the universal NoFI. Combine this within-model witness with univer
sal_nfi_any_substrate and thiele_represents_simulating_cert_sys
tem and you get the substrate-level statement. Take a certification system that satisfies
A2 and supplies a structure-preserving translation into the Thiele VM, packaged as a S
imulatingCertificationSystem, the record that carries the translation together
with a proof it commutes with steps. Its certifications then transport: an uncertified-
to-certified trace costs at least 1 in the source system, and the embedded execution
lands in a Thiele-certified state. The translation is a hypothesis the record supplies,
not something the theorem conjures for an arbitrary A2 system; the only Simulat
ingCertificationSystem the kernel constructs is the identity one (thiele_se
lf_simulating), and the cost floor for an A2 system with no embedding at all is
the direct content of universal_nfi_any_substrate. Its axiom closure is empty,
nothing named or assumed. What it does take is written in its type: the A2 rule, and
a run that starts uncertified and ends certified. The witness theorem shows what the
Thiele projection drops within the model. The substrate-independent theorem shows
that any substrate satisfying A2 inherits the µ-price on certification. Together they say:
the cost floor is forced wherever A2 is accepted. In Thiele, the dropped information


                                            117
The Thiele Machine                                            Devon Thiele • September 2026


sits in the state as a primitive field.

What would falsify this section. Not a clever function on the shadow. The six shadow
fields (vm_regs, vm_mem, vm_mu, vm_pc, vm_err, vm_certified) of s1 and s2 are
literally equal, so no function of them can tell the two apart. That part is reflexivity.
The part that can break is the model. If shadow_proj kept any piece of the graph,
or if the probe touched pg_morphisms, shadow_strictly_lossy would stop
compiling. The witness pair is small enough to check by hand. That is the seam this
section breaks at, and the place to go looking for it is Section 23.

   Plain reading. Same witness pair as Categorical Separation. The probe is a classical
   opcode that touches only the classical shadow. D3 says it leaves the morphism
   graph alone. So the graph-level difference survives the probe. The difference be-
   tween s1 and s2 isn’t an artifact of where they started. It persists through ordinary
   classical computation.


Theorem 14.2 (Shadow Separation). There exist two states with the same classical shadow
but different morphism graphs, and a legitimate graph-preserving probe after which the
morphism-level distinction remains.

Proof. Take s1 , s2 to be the Categorical-Separation witness pair: s1 has
pg_morphisms = [(0, id)], s2 has pg_morphisms = [], and the six
classical-shadow fields agree. So shadow_proj(s1 ) = shadow_proj(s2 ) but
s1 .vm_graph ̸= s2 .vm_graph.

The probe is the single-instruction program [instr_add 0 0 0 0] (ADD with δ = 0).
This instruction is classical: is_classical_opcode(instr_add 0 0 0 0) = true. By
D3 conservativity, the probe leaves vm_graph untouched on both s1 and s2 ; the Coq
proof checks it directly on the two witnesses (probe_preserves_graph_A, probe
_preserves_graph_B). After the probe the morphism lists are still [(0, id)] and [].
They still differ.

The probe used is deliberately boring: ADD 0 0 0 0. It is not a morphism operation.
That is the point. The theorem is not relying on a failing probe to manufacture a
difference. The difference is already in the structural state, and ordinary classical
projection cannot see it.


Corollary 14.3 (Classical observer is blind). No function of (registers, memory, µ, PC,
error, certified) can separate all VM states. The classical shadow is strictly lossy.


Corollary 14.4 (Thiele strictly extends classical). Every program in the classical fragment,
meaning every program restricted to is_classical_opcode, leaves pg_next_id un-
changed. There exist VM programs that change pg_next_id via PNEW. Therefore Thiele


                                            118
The Thiele Machine                                                Devon Thiele • September 2026


strictly extends the classical fragment: there is a start state and a PNEW step after which
pg_next_id differs, while every classical trace from that state leaves it unchanged. A uni-
versal Turing machine can simulate the full VM state by encoding it on tape, but a faithful
simulation has to preserve the µ-ledger cost. Drop the accounting and it is no longer simulating
the substrate honestly.


The embedding and projection results say which views survive and which structural
distinctions fall away. A projection that sends different graphs to the same place can’t
give you back the original graph on every state. That doesn’t rule out an encoding
that keeps the whole state. It doesn’t make these particular VM opcodes necessary in
every model, either.

15 The knowledge receipt: accounting and structural witnesses
Four short acts with one receipt. They show the difference between a morphism that
lives in the graph and a register that just holds its number, and they show the trace
recording what an assertion attempt cost, even when it fails. None of this vouches for
a receipt somebody else hands you, and none of it makes a property label true.

Act 1: The attempt costs, even when it fails.

Run MORPH_ASSERT(k, “test”, “”, 0) where morphism k does not exist. What happens:

   • vm_err becomes true (the assertion failed, morphism k not found).
   • csr_cert_addr stays 0, the cert-address channel is NOT activated on failure.
   • µ increases by S(0) = 1 regardless (the attempt cost something).

The VM charges for the failed existence check. That’s what MORPH_ASSERT guaran-
tees and all it guarantees: it checks the morphism exists, stores a checksum of the
property string, and leaves the certificate string unchecked.

Act 2: Build the chain.

Run this sequence:

                              PNEW( A), PNEW( B), PNEW(C )
                             MORPH( f , A, B), MORPH( g, B, C )
                                      COMPOSE(h, f , g)

Then MORPH_GET(r0 , h, source) and MORPH_GET(r1 , h, target). The resulting state has
r0 = A, r1 = C, and morphism h : A → C exists in the morphism map. With δ = 0
on each build step, µ = 0. The morphism exists, but no cert-address commitment has
been paid for yet.



                                             119
The Thiele Machine                                         Devon Thiele • September 2026


Act 3: Assert.

Run MORPH_ASSERT(h, “composed”, “”, 0). The assertion succeeds because mor-
phism h exists. The cert-address channel moves because the property string “com-
posed” has a non-NUL byte. csr_cert_addr goes to the ASCII checksum of “com-
posed” (nonzero). Cert-address channel activated. µ goes up by S(0) = 1.

(Why does the property string need a non-NUL byte? Because csr_cert_addr
stores the ASCII checksum of the property string, a plain byte-sum. The empty string
sums to 0, and so would an all-NUL string, leaving the channel at 0. Not activated.
Any string with a non-NUL byte gives a nonzero checksum and works. “composed”
is just an example label.)

The trace records a successful existence check and what it cost. The ledger can’t
go down under the VM’s step rule. The total by itself doesn’t say which assertion
happened or where. For that you need the trace. And keeping honest books is a
different job from catching an adversary who hands you a different history.

Act 4: Equal observations can hide different graphs.

Snapshot Program A at the end of Act 2, before Act 3’s MORPH_ASSERT. Its six-field
classical shadow is fixed: MORPH and COMPOSE wrote the new morphism IDs into their
destination registers and MORPH_GET read A and C back into r0 and r1 ; the program
counter sits at 8; µ = 0; error clear; uncertified.

Program B forges that shadow without building anything. It never runs MORPH or
COMPOSE. It just LOAD_IMMs the same values into the same registers (the morphism
IDs are deterministic, handed out in order (1, 2, 3) by pg_next_morph_id, which
boots at 1, so there is nothing secret to reproduce) and pads with δ = 0 CHECKPOINT
no-ops until its program counter also reads 8. Now every field the shadow keeps
matches Program A’s, µ = 0 on both sides. A classical observer cannot tell them
apart. That two states can share the entire shadow while differing in the graph is the
Categorical Separation Theorem (Section 14); Program B is the forged half of exactly
such a pair.

The whole difference lives in the graph. Program A built morphism h with MORPH
and COMPOSE, so h sits in its pg_morphisms. Program B’s register h holds the num-
ber 3, but its graph is empty: the value is a forged token, not a graph entry. Apply
MORPH_DELETE(h): Program A succeeds, because there is a morphism to delete. Pro-
gram B fails and sets err, because there is not. MORPH_DELETE reads the graph, and
the graph is the one thing LOAD_IMM cannot forge.

The ledger is what the schedule added up to. The graph carries the structure the
shadow leaves out. The trace tells you where things came from, as long as the verifier
has a history it can trust.



                                         120
The Thiele Machine                                                        Devon Thiele • September 2026



16 The VM, built three times: proof, runner, hardware
Three scaffolding layers wrap the substrate: a Coq kernel, an OCaml runner ex-
tracted from it, and a Kami RTL design tied back to it. None of them IS the sub-
strate. They are how the substrate gets to live on a chip, on a laptop, and inside a
proof checker. Here is where proof stops. Coq proves that the Kami model steps the
way vm_apply does (driven_step_wf, driven_trace_commutes), under the
WFDrivenPrecondition and WFDrivenRun premises. Separate theorems cover
retirement under the real priority scheduler. That is the proof. Past the Kami model,
the Bluespec compiler, the printer, synthesis, place-and-route, and the bitstream are
tested or trusted, not proved. The extracted OCaml runner is checked by parity tests.
The three layers below go through it edge by edge. The seams are named in the source,
out in the open, so you can walk to any one of them and see what it actually rests on.
I would rather hand you the exact seam where proof stops and tested correspondence
starts than dress the whole stack up as one seamless theorem it is not.

                                                      Coq kernel
                                            0 Admitted, 0 project-local axioms

                        OCaml runner                                              Kami RTL

                                                                                          BSC compile
                                  parity tests
                                                                                          trust boundary


                        Python harness                                             Bluespec

                                                                                          yosys


                                                                                 Verilog netlist

                                                                                          nextpnr-xilinx


                                                                            Xilinx Kintex-7 FPGA

                                                                                   bitstream


Figure 6: Three ways the substrate gets built. The Coq kernel is the source of truth.
The left branch goes to a program you can run; the right branch goes to silicon. Blue
arrows are either Coq-model correspondences, conditional, with the theorem named
on the edge, or tests. Red arrows and the tool steps below them are places where a
translation or a tool is trusted, unless a separate theorem or artifact says otherwise.
Nothing here claims one proof running end to end from Coq to the bitstream.



                                                  121
The Thiele Machine                                            Devon Thiele • September 2026


16.1 Layer 1: The Coq kernel
Coq is where the substrate gets pinned down. The kernel defines:

   • The VMState record (all 12 fields, as in Section 11).
   • The vm_instruction type: an inductive type (Section A) with 51 constructors,
     one for each opcode, each carrying the right argument types.
   • The vm_apply function: takes a state and an instruction, returns a state. It is
     total (Section A): every input pair produces a defined output, including the error
     case.
   • All the theorems: No Free Insight, µ-monotonicity, µ-initiality, categorical sepa-
     ration, Turing strictness, and the rest.

The Coq kernel operates mostly over natural numbers, but VMState is not an un-
bounded mathematical fantasy. REG_COUNT = 16. MEM_SIZE = 128. Both are
matched against the synthesised RTL. The accessors wrap indices through those sizes
(modular arithmetic, Section A). Both are plain definitions, and the proofs are stated
for these values; no theorem instantiates other ones. Register and memory writes
are truncated through the kernel’s 64-bit word helpers to keep the math clean. The µ
counter is a plain natural number with no fixed upper bound. The RTL instantiates
a finite 32-bit datapath (the part of the chip that moves and operates on data, not the
control logic) and bounded tables.

Arithmetic near a word boundary is where a bridge claim can quietly stop meaning
what it says, so state it concretely. ADD on the register values 232 − 1 and 1: 32-bit mod-
ular arithmetic wraps the sum to 0; the kernel’s 64-bit word truncation instead gives
232 , a different value. This is not a hypothetical; it is exactly the gap between the RTL’s
32-bit datapath and the kernel’s 64-bit word helpers named in the paragraph above.
Values whose sum stays under 232 agree on both sides; values whose sum crosses
that boundary do not, and the bridge domain has to say which side is authoritative
for such a value or exclude it. The µ counter’s own range needs the same treatment
rather than an unqualified “unbounded natural on both sides” reading: the kernel’s
µ is a plain unbounded natural number, but the RTL ledger is a fixed-width register,
so a program that accumulates enough µ to exceed that width is outside the proved
hardware correspondence, the same way an oversized ADD is.

Zero Admitted means: no step in any theorem proof was asserted without derivation.
Coq checked everything. I did not.

16.2 Layer 2: The OCaml extracted runner
Here is the trick that makes the OCaml layer worth having at all. A Coq definition is
two things wearing one coat: a recipe for computing an answer, and a proof that the
recipe behaves. Extraction takes the coat off. It keeps the recipe, in this case rendered
into OCaml, an ordinary programming language you can compile and run like any

                                            122
The Thiele Machine                                            Devon Thiele • September 2026


other, and it drops the proof terms on the floor, because a CPU has no use for them.
So vm_apply (the function that defines what every opcode does) walks out the door
as an OCaml function that computes the same answer on the same inputs. The proofs
do not come along. The algorithm does. That is the whole point: I get a binary I can
actually run, descended from the exact definition the theorems were proved about,
and the descent is mechanical rather than me retyping the semantics into a second
language and praying. The extracted runner executes all 51 opcodes from the Coq
semantics, morphism operations and CHSH_LASSERT included.

The trust boundary at this layer is named explicitly. The kernel proves formal facts
about the Coq-side observable (shadow_to_eo (vm_apply s i) repackages the
full post-state as an observable record after one instruction): the certification floor,
where a false-to-true flip raises the observable µ by at least 1, and µ never decreas-
ing (ocaml_observable_nofi_and_monotone). It also names the external run-
ner boundary. The Coq lemma ocaml_runner_observable_defined only says
the observable is defined, which is true of any Coq function. It is not an unproved
Hypothesis (Section A), and it is not a cross-language equivalence theorem. The
cross-language claim, that the built OCaml binary returns the same observable as the
Coq semantics on every input, is checked by the parity test suite (Section A: run both
on the same battery, verify they agree). I do not count that as a kernel theorem. It is
an audited boundary, backed by tests, named in the source.

16.3 Layer 3: The Kami RTL hardware
The whole reason this layer exists is to refuse to retype the design in a second language.
Kami lets you write a hardware specification inside Coq itself and then extract it, the
same move the OCaml runner used, except the target is not software, it is a chip. The
first stop is Bluespec SystemVerilog, a hardware description language pitched high
enough that you say what the hardware should compute rather than which wire goes
where: the registers it holds, the rules it runs, and the conditions under which each
state transition is allowed to fire. The Bluespec compiler then drops a level and emits
standard Verilog RTL, the gate-and-register description that synthesis tools chew into
actual logic, either on an FPGA, a chip you can reconfigure after it leaves the factory, or
an ASIC, a chip burned for one design and never reprogrammable. So a Kami module
is the same idea descending one more flight of stairs: the finite hardware realisation,
pinned down in Coq, walks all the way to gates without anyone hand-translating it
on the way, and the Kami extractor runs it through the pipeline above.

The hardware has:

   • 16 registers, each 32 bits wide (RegCount = 16; the kernel fixes REG_COUNT
     = 16)
   • 128-bit instruction encoding (InstrSz = 128)
   • 128 words of data memory and 128 instruction words (MemSize = 128; the


                                           123
The Thiele Machine                                          Devon Thiele • September 2026


      kernel fixes MEM_SIZE = 128)
   • bounded instruction transport and execution state
   • 64 partition module slots (PTableSz = 64)
   • 16 slots in each of the morphism, coupling, formula, certificate, descriptor-
     metadata, and coupling-pair tables
   • An on-chip LASSERT FSM for formula verification (with 64-entry inlined back-
     ing buffers)
   • The µ ledger updated atomically with each executed instruction (no separate
     parallel datapath; the cost arithmetic happens inside the same step rule)

The Verilog is the hardware artifact. The FPGA is whatever I could put it on for
free. The current target is the Xilinx Kintex-7 xc7k325tffg900-2 on the Digilent
Genesys 2: the smallest fully-open-toolchain part the design fits comfortably, the multi-
cycle CHSH_LASSERT witness checker included. Section A explains the synthesis
pipeline I use: yosys does the gate-level synthesis, nextpnr-xilinx does the place-
and-route, and prjxray’s xc7frames2bit produces the bitstream the chip reads to
configure itself.

CI (Full) runs synthesis, simulation, and bitstream generation. I don’t own a Genesys 2.
CI routes the design and writes the bitstream, and no board has loaded it. A resource,
timing, or bitstream report only counts for the exact RTL and tool versions recorded
with it. Compiling the Coq proofs doesn’t measure changed RTL. Any physical claim
about the current design needs freshly regenerated pipeline artifacts tied to the source
identity in Section 21.1.

The CPU comes out of reset halted. Nothing runs until a program is in. The program
comes over the board’s USB serial line, and the loader that takes it in is written in
Kami too, so it comes out of the same extraction as the CPU. Nobody hand-writes
it in Verilog. ThieleLoader reads the pin through two synchronizing registers,
assembles sixteen bytes into each instruction word, hands each word to the CPU’s
loadInstr method, and calls start after the last one. When the CPU halts or faults,
the loader sends back fifteen bytes: the status, the program counter, the µ ledger, and
the error code.

Coq checks that chain one link at a time. CPUStart.start_from_reset says one
start call from reset gives exactly the state every dispatch theorem starts from. Loa
derSerial.one_frame runs the receiver cycle by cycle as the Kami method itself
and says one frame of any byte comes out as exactly that byte, once. LoaderSeria
l.serial_program_load says that for any program of 1 to 128 instructions, after
the frames of the count and of instructions 0 through k, the loader holds instruction
k at address k, and after the last one it asks for the start. LoaderSerial.load_ru
le_hands_off and LoaderSerial.load_instr_writes say the rule that takes



                                          124
The Thiele Machine                                         Devon Thiele • September 2026


a held word calls loadInstr with exactly that word and address, and loadInstr
writes it into instruction memory.

One link isn’t proved. Kami’s semantics say a rule may fire whenever its guard holds.
They don’t say when it fires. So Coq doesn’t prove that the loader’s load rule takes
each word before the next word replaces it. Each word stays held for at least fifteen
more frames, and the simulation tests (scripts/system_sim.py, on the extracted
Verilog and on a gate netlist) check that whole programs land and run.

The kernel fixes REG_COUNT and MEM_SIZE at these values, and no theorem is stated
for other ones. Hardware realises one choice of constants. The size constants (16
registers, 128 data words, 128 instruction words) were chosen small enough to fit on a
free-toolchain FPGA.

47 of the 51 opcodes are implemented in the synthesized RTL: the Kami core has
hardware logic for those 47, and the synthesised Verilog carries them through to the
FPGA bitstream. The remaining four (the Q1+AB check-opcode family instr_chsh_l
assert_1ab, instr_chsh_lassert_1ab_g5, instr_chsh_lassert_1ab_g3
45, instr_chsh_lassert_1ab_g12345) are defined in the Kami HW abstraction
with kernel-equivalence proven, and they execute in the OCaml runner and Python
harness, but they are not synthesized into the FPGA bitstream. Each of them would
need its own wide-arithmetic FSM, and I haven’t built that hardware. The OCaml/RTL
opcode-set parity test tolerates exactly these four. Which opcodes are in hardware
isn’t the real question. The real question is which opcodes have a completed formal
bisimulation proof.

The    theorem     KamiHW.GraphReconstructionBridge.driv
en_step_wf           compares      the     Gallina     function    kami_step     with
Kernel.SimulationProof.vm_apply                   through       abs_full_snapshot.
Its premise is WFDrivenPrecondition ks i.                        The trace theorem
driven_trace_commutes requires WFDrivenRun fuel trace ks, which
checks that premise at every visited state. This is the intermediate Coq model surface,
separate from physical clock transitions in ThieleCPUCore.v.

   • The default branch of WFDrivenPrecondition is True. It adds no instruction-
     specific premise to this intermediate model equality.
   • CALL and RET require WellFormedSnapshot, with a PC bound for CALL.
     CHSH_TRIAL requires Boolean operands. LASSERT requires agreement with
     its memory header. Tensor access requires valid indices, and TENSOR_GET also
     requires module existence.
   • MORPH requires extended_hw_invariant and existing source and target
     modules. COMPOSE, MORPH_TENSOR, and MORPH_GET require the ex-
     tended invariant. MORPH_ID uses the empty-descriptor invariant and module
     existence. MORPH_ASSERT and MORPH_DELETE require morph-table well-


                                         125
The Thiele Machine                                          Devon Thiele • September 2026


      formedness.
   • PNEW requires a nonempty normalized region and fresh tensor state. PSPLIT
     and PMERGE carry the partition, capacity, freshness, and arithmetic conditions
     in their source branches. These per-operation conditions must be established
     along the actual run; reset alone does not provide unlimited table capacity.

The actual CPU rules also have executable Kami semantics. CoreRules places all
twelve rules in the supported linear-action fragment; CoreExecution proves that
finite traces of its first-enabled selector are actual Kami multi-step executions, with
a firing bound and an early-stop characterization by absence of enabled rules. Re-
set has an enabled selected rule. CoreTyping proves preservation of the complete
register-name/kind schema from reset, while DispatchReset states thirteen typed
initialization facts. So the selected execution and the register types are pinned down.
Fairness isn’t, and neither are the invariants about what gets allocated along a reach-
able run, the full abstract observation relation, or meaning surviving the trip through
the RTL toolchain.

Carrying results all the way to finite RTL takes more: matching word ranges, memory
addressing, allocation capacity, reset state, and a view taken when an instruction
retires. The word-boundary example above is one case of it. Exact equality needs a
range restriction or a proved modular abstraction, and the finite ledger needs a bound
that rules out overflow. Multi-cycle operations need a retirement relation that accounts
for the FSM’s in-between states.

The coupling FSM reads serialized MORPH pairs and implements pair copying and
relational joins for the other two operations. Descriptor zero remains reserved for
empty/identity couplings. The finite pair table holds 16 entries; insufficient space
raises an error, and empty results need no pair entries. The RTL regression tests
inspect the backing pair tables through the testbench. They do not establish full-state
refinement: hardware lacks the software coupling-label field and module-membership
filter, and the full endpoint/region representation contract remains an obligation for a
broader physical correspondence claim. The RTL normalizes the newly allocated pair
range, retaining each pair’s last occurrence as the software’s nodup does. Allocation
first requires room for the raw intermediate list; a normalized result fitting the table
does not guarantee admission when the raw list exceeds it.

The inventory arithmetic rtl_inventory_arithmetic is not a proof of these
representation obligations. The 47 synthesized / four nonsynthesized distinction
describes the implementation inventory; it does not turn a conditional model theorem
into unrestricted RTL equality.

And the four Q1+AB check opcodes are not stranded by sitting outside that partition:
their kernel-equivalence proof makes them sound at the substrate level (see §18), and
they run in the OCaml runner and Python harness. What they lack is a synthesized-


                                          126
The Thiele Machine                                           Devon Thiele • September 2026


Verilog counterpart to bisimulate against, which is why they sit outside the 47 instead
of inside it as a gap. That’s honest proof accounting. Nothing is papered over.

The substrate is the math. The Coq kernel pins it down. The hardware is a finite
physical instantiation with explicit table sizes and word widths. The proofs state the
bridge between them under the finite representation invariants where those invariants
are needed. This is the only honest relationship physical hardware can have with an
abstract mathematical substrate.

Substrate-level vs program-level enforcement. In the Coq kernel, A2 is part of the
step relation. A2 says: flipping certification false-to-true costs at least 1, at the step
level. Look at the cases for CERTIFY and MORPH_ASSERT in vm_apply: the cost is
added before the flag flips; the step that mutates the certification channel and the step
that charges µ are the same step. That is what A2 means in the kernel.

A correct conventional simulator can keep the VM’s invariant on the states it encodes.
A different or buggy simulator might not. That doesn’t prove anything about what con-
ventional computation can’t do. The VM theorem is about the VM’s own step relation.
Carrying it into hardware also takes the representation conditions in WFDrivenRun
and the documented extraction and compiler trust boundaries.

Enforcing the law, representing it, and seeing it are separate questions, and I keep
them separate. The abstract A2 theorem allows any state type and any designated
predicate. Exact unit pricing is a stronger condition, relative to how many certification
flips happen. A2 itself allows larger charges. And neither one vouches for a receipt
from outside without some evidence to back it.

17 Five minimal models inspired by other disciplines
Five other fields need a commitment to stick. The abstract records leave the kind of
state and instruction open, so you can drop in something that looks nothing like this
VM. Here are five small models, each borrowed from one of those fields. Coq checks
each model and what follows from it. Whether a real deployed protocol behaves like
its model is a separate claim, and it needs its own evidence.

Ordinary machines can do all of this. That is the point of the section. Blockchains, gas
meters, attestation, transparency logs, and proof-carrying code all run on conventional
hardware. Four of them keep records that are meant to last; proof-carrying code does
not. What the four share is permanence, not a price on the write. Read against their
actual specifications (Section 17.7), only one prices writing its record, and it refunds
the write when the record is taken back before it settles. The others hold their records
in place by pricing reversal or by building them so they can’t be undone. The claim of
this book is about the account of computation: it should carry the record and its price,
so the next field doesn’t have to build them again. It does not say every field already
prices the write, and it does not say a machine can’t carry one.


                                           127
The Thiele Machine                                          Devon Thiele • September 2026


17.1 Proof-of-stake finality
Stake it or it doesn’t count. The model has a yes/no finalized flag, and finalizing
puts stake at risk. A transition that finalizes for zero breaks A2. Charging at least one
unit on it satisfies A2. What the model records is exposure to loss. It doesn’t say the
collateral was actually destroyed, or that each exposure is a separate unit spent, or
that every real consensus protocol works this way. The file has no Casper checkpoints,
quorum weights, double or surround votes, or accountable-safety theorem.

In the model, nothing_at_stake_is_free_forgery rules out an A2 instance for
the free-finalization witness it’s handed. Under the slashing-schedule premise, sl
ashing_finality_floor says any run that starts unfinalized and ends finalized
carries at least one unit of modeled charge. Using this on a real protocol means first
showing its transitions match the model’s.

Falsifier: a transition system satisfying the stated slashing-schedule and execution
premises with an initially unfinalized, finally finalized trace whose modeled charge
sum is zero.

17.2 Gas metering
Paying for the commitment and paying for everything else are different bills. The
gas model counts commitments, one unit each. gas_schedule_exactness says
exactly when a schedule prices those commitments exactly: meeting the floor and
never overcharging pins down both which steps get charged and the unit price, and
exact pricing gives you those two conditions back. A real gas schedule also charges
for things like computation and storage. Its full fee isn’t this counter, and no EVM
opcode table is modeled here.

The model keeps two failures apart: leaving out a mandatory charge, and charging
more than the reference counter. undercharged_opcode_admits_free_comm
itment concerns a committing step charged zero. overcharge_breaks_exact
ness concerns the no-overcharge condition. The VM has an exact state-dependent
commitment pricer, thiele_vm_commit_pricing_is_exact, bounded above by
its full ledger schedule via thiele_unit_price_lower_bounds_mu. Repeated
CERTIFY instructions illustrate the distinction: each pays the instruction floor, while
only a false-to-true transition contributes to the flip counter.

Falsifier: a schedule satisfying floor and no-overcharge whose charging predicate
differs from the commitment predicate on some reachable step.

17.3 Trusted-execution attestation
Show the measurement, or the verifier can’t tell. The attestation model adds a mea-
surement field to the report. Under its coverage and evidence assumptions, atte
station_cannot_factor_through_bare_transcript rules out a sound and
complete verifier that only reads the bare transcript. replay_is_a_two_preima
ge_witness supplies the model’s collision: two runs, one bare transcript. That is

                                          128
The Thiele Machine                                          Devon Thiele • September 2026


the shape of a replay, not a replay attack; no signed report or freshness rule is in the
model. measurement_enriched_attestation_succeeds gives a verifier that
works on the enriched reports. The point is that some distinguishing evidence has
to be there. It doesn’t have to be any particular physical register, and nothing here
vouches for a measurement an adversary hands over.

Falsifier: a sound and complete attestation scheme for a µ-dependent claim that reads
only the bare transcript.

The TPM quote file is a deliberately scoped abstraction of fields from TPM 2.0 Library
Version 185. It fixes one epoch-initial PCR value to zero as a model choice, folds
abstract extends into that PCR, and represents a quote by the selected-PCR digest
and verifier nonce. Reset, restart, signature computation, and signature verification
are outside the model; quote authenticity is assumed. A verifier reading only these
modeled quote fields cannot decide an additional Boolean runtime flag that the quote
definition ignores (quote_cannot_attest_unmeasured_state). A quote reports
selected PCR state, not arbitrary unmeasured runtime state. The theorem does not
establish a generic boot-time-attestation result or discover a TPM vulnerability.

The application to V_does_not_factor_through_classical is explicit in qu
ote_runtime_verifier_separation. The adapter stores both modeled quote
fields in a classical transcript, with no information lost (quote_projection_faith
ful). The two VM witness states label the two truth values of the runtime claim. This
transport makes no assertion that TPM cost or energy equals µ.

17.4 Certificate transparency
Keep a log, or live with the split view. The transparency model adds an inclusion-
verdict bit, governed by the same exact disclosure contract as the commitment route.
abstract_log_bit_verifier builds a sound and complete verifier under that
contract. abstract_bare_verifier_impossible rules out the bare verifier for
the VM claim, and abstract_mu_collision_witness is the colliding pair. The
pair has a split view’s shape; an RFC split view is two inconsistent log histories, and
that is not what the pair is. Saying these fields are a real certificate-transparency
deployment would take its own implementation and security argument.

Falsifier: a sound and complete log-free verifier with the same soundness target.

17.5 Proof-carrying verification
Carry the proof along. The proof-carrying model puts evidence in a transcript field
called rounds. proof_rounds_escape verifies that field under its evidence contract,
and bare_pcc_impossible is what happens when the evidence is taken away.
Calling the field rounds doesn’t make a one-shot certificate interactive. The evidence
is one number the contract says equals µ; no code, safety policy, or proof checker is
modeled. Separately, level_k_verification_floor, which is just another name
for level_k_certification_cost_floor, says a run from the clean start that


                                          129
The Thiele Machine                                             Devon Thiele • September 2026


ends certified after executing one CERTIFY charged at least k costs at least k, and l
evel_k_verification_floor_tight shows it can be exactly k. That count is
a different thing from the per-instruction charge levels in Section 19. Neither one
measures prover time, circuit size, or proof length.

Falsifier: a level-k certified trace with total µ below k, or a sound and complete zero-
round bare verifier.

17.7 Five specifications, read through the record axis
The models above are mine. Here are the specifications themselves, read with three
questions: what is the record, what keeps it permanent, and where does a price sit. A
price can sit at three levels. It can be economic, a fee or a deposit the protocol takes. It
can be cryptographic, a reversal made infeasible by a hardness assumption. Or it can
be physical, energy spent under Landauer’s principle.




                                            130
The Thiele Machine                                               Devon Thiele • September 2026
