Ethereum is the one that prices the write. Even there the price holds only for a record
that outlives the transaction. Write a slot and clear it before the transaction ends, and
the refund gives nearly all of it back. That is the finite-hardware result’s shape again:
the write that can be revoked is the one that goes free.

Casper prices the other side. Finalizing a block need not slash anybody (finaliza
tion_without_slashing exhibits one such finalization). Reversing it does: two
finalized blocks on different branches mean a “1/3” set of validators broke a slashing
rule. That is accountable safety, ported from the Casper verification team’s Coq model
and proved constructively here, without excluded middle. The theorem account
able_safety and the record-reading consequence conflicting_records_are
_priced are closed under the global context. Their domain is the abstract Casper
setting (quorum intersection, votes, justification, finalization, and slashing), not every
deployed Ethereum configuration. It protects what A2 protects, permanence, from the
opposite end. It is not an instance of A2, and it is not a counterexample to it. It is a
second way to hold a record in place.

TPM and CT price nothing in their specifications. A hash chain or an append-only log
stays put because undoing it is infeasible, which is a cryptographic guarantee, not an
exact one. A PCR’s record lasts until the platform resets it, so its permanence is per
boot. There is a physical cost underneath: a register that exactly kept a permanent
record on finite hardware would have to merge states (Section 9.1). The specifications
don’t price it.



                                              131
The Thiele Machine                                           Devon Thiele • September 2026


Proof-carrying code keeps no permanent record and prices nothing. It is outside the
family.

   Plain reading. All five need their commitments to count. Four keep permanent
   records, and they hold them in place three ways: a price on the write, a price on
   reversal, or a structure that can’t be undone. Only one matches A2’s exact form.

Whether permanence is always paid for depends on the level. At the logical level it
always is: on a finite state space, a step that permanently switches a record on cannot
be injective (permanent_write_has_logical_payment). An economic, crypto-
graphic, or physical payment needs a premise about that level, such as Landauer’s
principle for heat, and holds exactly where the premise holds.

18 Binary Game Statistics: where a Priced Check Meets the NPA Boundary
This section is about one build and one bound. The abstract model has no CHSH step;
the VM has one, realised with two opcodes at level 1 (CHSH_TRIAL, CHSH_LASSERT)
and extended by four Q1+AB assertion opcodes below, and it is here as a stress test of
the cost law against a boundary I did not write. CHSH_TRIAL records outcomes of
a 2-player binary game into hardware witness counters. CHSH_LASSERT refuses to
advance normally unless those counters satisfy a Z-arithmetic check.

The enforcement doesn’t care who built the wall. CHSH_LASSERT refuses any tally
outside one fixed algebraic slice by trapping to LASSERT_TRAP_PC, and every super-
quantum correlation is outside it (so are some classical ones; the section says which);
LASSERT refuses a formula whose dual witness fails to exclude a single valuation by
trapping to the same address, the same way. One wall comes from the NPA mathemat-
ics, which I did not author and could not have, and it sits inside the Tsirelson bound.
The other is a logical constraint I wrote down myself. The step traps the same on both.
So µ isn’t pricing contact with external structure as some special case. It prices the
certified narrowing as such, and the trap fires the same whether the state it throws out
is a PR box or a falsifying assignment I handed it. Insight costs, and the price never
asks whether you found the wall or built it.

And the claim is about that step. The chip is something else. Some cert-opcodes have
no synthesised FPGA implementation. That does not change the transition theorem.
The trap is the cost-on-certification law, and the law lives in the transition, not in the
gate count.

Everything below earns that. The matrix algebra is the discharge: it shows that ev-
erything CHSH_LASSERT lets through sits inside the Tsirelson bound, a bar I did not
set myself. I came at it sideways, stress-testing the machine’s priced checks against a
known algebraic boundary, the NPA moment matrix conditions for what correlations
are achievable under polynomial constraints.



                                           132
The Thiele Machine                                            Devon Thiele • September 2026


I did not set out to find what I found. The chain is concrete. The Z-arithmetic check
the kernel runs on the witness counters (decidable, integer-only) is sound with respect
to the real-valued column-contractive predicate by column_contractive_check
_witness_sound, a 188-line proof. That real-valued predicate is biconditional with
PSD of the specified zero-marginal, zero-within-party-moment matrix by column_c
ontractive_iff_npa_psd. End-to-end: a successful CHSH_LASSERT step entails
PSD of that specified matrix on the witness-derived correlators, by chsh_lassert
_no_trap_implies_npa_psd. No quantum-mechanical premises live in the Coq
dependency tree: no Hilbert space, no operators, no observables. The identification of
that PSD condition with quantum-realizable correlations lives in the NPA framework
itself (Navascués–Pironio–Acín 2007, 2008). I cite it. I do not derive it. The kernel’s
content is the algebraic bridge between an integer-arithmetic check inside an opcode
and PSD of a specific matrix. The theorem names say PSD, because PSD is what the
kernel proves.

Which way it runs: the machine first, NPA second. Two passes, in this order. The first
pass stays on the machine’s side and never mentions NPA: the CHSH_TRIAL opcode,
what its witness counters cost, the integer check column_contractive_check_w
itness that CHSH_LASSERT runs on those counters, and the proof that the integer
check is sound for its real-number version. What the step checks at CHSH_LASSERT
comes down to three polynomial inequalities on cleared integer ratios of the counters.
The ledger charges for running that check; it does not produce the check. The test is
defined inside the step rule and checked by the kernel without looking anything up in
the quantum-mechanics literature. The second pass is where the matrix comes in. Lift
the machine’s own test to the reals (IZR, with the witness counts as denominators) and
you get the column-contractive predicate, and that predicate holds exactly when the
specified zero-marginal, zero-within-party-moment matrix is PSD. Both sides of that
biconditional are defined mathematical predicates, and the proof is pure polynomial
arithmetic with no project-local axioms. It isn’t a claim that the runtime built a physical
quantum experiment. The result that carries the weight is the subsection “Algebraic
characterization of the CHSH gate condition.” The subsections before it (the Bell
setup, the classical bound, the quantum bound) are the walk there. If you have time
for one subsection, that’s the one. The next subsection builds the game from scratch
for anyone who hasn’t met Bell tests; if you have, skip ahead.

18.1 The Bell test setup, from scratch
Imagine two players, Alice and Bob, in separate rooms. They cannot communicate
once the game starts. A referee sends Alice one of two questions (x ∈ {0, 1}). The
referee sends Bob one of two questions (y ∈ {0, 1}). Alice answers a ∈ {0, 1}. Bob
answers b ∈ {0, 1}. (Settings ( x, y), outcomes ( a, b): same convention as Section 11
and the Coq.)

The game rule: they want a ⊕ b = x ∧ y. The symbol ⊕ is XOR (exclusive OR):


                                           133
The Thiele Machine                                                                Devon Thiele • September 2026


a ⊕ b = 1 when a ̸= b, and 0 when a = b. The symbol ∧ is AND: x ∧ y = 1 only
when both x = 1 and y = 1, otherwise 0. In plain language: their answers should
be different if and only if both questions were 1. For all other question combinations
(both 0, or one each), they should match.

                                         x             Referee                se
                                                                                 tti
                                     g                                               ng
                                  in
                             sett                                                       y




                     Alice                                                                  Bob
                                                       shared λ
                 separated room                                                    separated room




             outcome a ∈ {0, 1}                                              outcome b ∈ {0, 1}
                                                    no communication
                                                     during the game

                                              Win condition: a ⊕ b = x ∧ y


Figure 7: The CHSH setup. The referee distributes settings x, y; Alice and Bob each
have a separated room and may share an agreed-on λ from before the game started.
No communication during the game. Each produces a binary outcome (0/1 on the
wire, recoded as ±1 for the correlators). The four correlators Exy = ⟨ ab⟩, read in the
±1 encoding, are what the kernel’s CHSH_LASSERT instruction checks against the
column-contractivity / NPA condition.


Why 75% is the classical ceiling. Before the game, Alice and Bob can agree on any
deterministic strategy: a function a( x, λ) for Alice and b(y, λ) for Bob, where λ is any
shared randomness they agree on in advance. Once the game starts, no communica-
tion. One assumption, said out loud: the referee asks the four question pairs equally
often. That is where the 75% comes from. Drop it and the exact statement is only that
every deterministic strategy loses at least one of the four pairs.

Fix any deterministic strategy and freeze λ. Then Alice has two predetermined an-
swers, a0 and a1 , and Bob has two predetermined answers, b0 and b1 . Winning all four
question combinations would require:


                a0 ⊕ b0 = 0,                 a0 ⊕ b1 = 0,    a1 ⊕ b0 = 0,         a1 ⊕ b1 = 1.

Now XOR the four left sides. Every variable appears twice, so the total is 0. XOR
the four right sides and the total is 1. That is impossible. So every deterministic local
strategy loses at least one of the four cases, and the best deterministic strategy wins at
most 3 out of 4 question combinations.

And you cannot buy your way past it with a coin flip. A randomized strategy is


                                                          134
The Thiele Machine                                                  Devon Thiele • September 2026


nothing but a bag of deterministic ones with a die deciding which to pull, and every
strategy in the bag already tops out at 75%. Average a pile of things that each sit at
75% or below and the average sits at 75% or below; there is no arrangement of losers
that sums to a winner. That is linearity of expectation, the most boring theorem in the
building, quietly closing the last door.

So under that uniform-setting contract the classical ceiling is 3/4, seventy-five percent,
and not a hair more. The kernel states the same ceiling in its own units, |S| ≤ 2, and
pins that down both ways with nothing Admitted; 75% is that bound read as a win
rate under the uniform-setting contract, and the reading is the argument above, not
a Coq statement. One executable trace, built from nothing but PNEW, PSPLIT, and
CHSH_TRIAL and never spending a cent of the cost ledger (µ = 0), records a tally with
S = 2 and touches the ceiling. Then local_box_CHSH_bound closes the lid from
the other side: no factorizable box, a finite mixture of deterministic local strategies,
gets |S| over 2. A trace can of course just write any tallies it likes into the counters at
µ = 0, the way anyone can write a fake scoreboard; the theorem is about what local
play can earn, and the certification step that would stand behind a scoreboard is the
priced one. The achievable and the unbeatable land on the same number, which is the
only kind of ceiling I trust.

18.2 The CHSH inequality: where it comes from
Now here is what took me a while to understand: why is there a specific algebraic
combination S that detects whether correlations can be explained classically?

The key move is this. For any fixed shared randomness λ, Alice’s output A x (λ) ∈
{−1, +1} (switching to the ±1 encoding now instead of 0/1, indexed by Alice’s setting
x) and Bob’s By (λ) ∈ {−1, +1} (indexed by Bob’s setting y). Consider the expression:


                A0 (λ) B0 (λ) + A0 (λ) B1 (λ) + A1 (λ) B0 (λ) − A1 (λ) B1 (λ)

Factor it:


                     = A0 (λ)( B0 (λ) + B1 (λ)) + A1 (λ)( B0 (λ) − B1 (λ))

Since B0 and B1 are each ±1, either B0 + B1 = ±2 and B0 − B1 = 0, or B0 + B1 = 0 and
B0 − B1 = ±2. The two terms cannot both be large. In either case, | B0 + B1 | + | B0 −
B1 | = 2, and since | A0 |, | A1 | ≤ 1, the entire expression is at most 2 in absolute value
for any fixed λ.

Average over λ:


                         S = ⟨ A0 B0 ⟩ + ⟨ A0 B1 ⟩ + ⟨ A1 B0 ⟩ − ⟨ A1 B1 ⟩


                                               135
The Thiele Machine                                           Devon Thiele • September 2026


where ⟨ A x By ⟩ is the correlation (expected value of the product) when settings are
x and y. Since the expression before averaging was bounded by 2 for every λ, the
average satisfies |S| ≤ 2.

That is the argument. The combination with the minus sign is chosen exactly because
the factoring trick works: one of the B-terms goes to zero and the other to ±2, forcing
the whole thing below 2. The inequality |S| ≤ 2 is a pure algebraic consequence of the
outputs being ±1 and being determined locally.

None of that lives only in my head. The argument I just walked you through is
the English proof; the formal check that I did not fool myself anywhere is loca
l_box_CHSH_bound, which says the plain thing in symbols: for any factorizable
box B, |S( B)| ≤ 2, with nothing left Admitted. And a bound from above is only
half an honest claim. classical_bound_achieved hands over the other half, a
constructive witness, an executable trace at µ = 0 that actually reaches |S| = 2 rather
than just respecting it. Put the two together and the ceiling is not loose: |S| ≤ 2 for
every factorizable box, and a µ = 0 VM trace records a tally with S = 2.
                                   √
18.3 The quantum bound: why 2 2?
Now hand the same game to quantum mechanics and the ceiling lifts. Not to anything
                                                             √
you please, and not all the way to a perfect 4; it lifts to 2 2 ≈ 2.828 and stops there as
hard as the classical 2 stopped. That number is the Tsirelson bound, named for Boris
                                                        √
Tsirelson, who was the first to write down |S| ≤ 2 2 and pin quantum correlations
under it. The physics story of why it is that exact number, and not some other irrational
nobody would have guessed, runs through Hilbert-space operators, anticommutators,
the Pauli matrices, the Bloch sphere, the whole machinery. It is a real and beautiful
derivation and I am going to walk straight past it, because none of the formal content
here leans on it. I do not need to retell quantum mechanics to show you the kernel
respects the wall; I only need the algebra, and that is what I am keeping.

What the formal content does depend on, and what the next subsections derive, is the
algebra. One bridge theorem takes PSD of the zero-marginal NPA matrix as its premise.
Reading that premise as quantum realizability is the NPA framework’s step, and it
                                                                         √
is not derived in Coq. Its conclusion is the algebraic bound |S| ≤ 2 2. The “What
the algebra proves” subsection below then builds the same bound from polynomial
inequalities on the specified moment matrix without any quantum-physics premise at
all. That is the path I follow, and the formal claim is the algebraic one.
                                  √
And that gap between 2 and 2 2 is not bookkeeping. It is the most stubborn fact
in physics: people have gone into labs, fired entangled photons through polarizers,
counted coincidences, and watched the number sit up above 2 where no local classical
story is allowed to put it. Whatever the world is doing, it is not Alice and Bob with
                                                                                  √
a shared cheat sheet agreed beforehand. The physics of why the wall sits at 2 2
is somebody else’s chapter and a good one. My question is narrower and, to me,
stranger: why does an integer check on counters stay under a wall it never names?

                                           136
The Thiele Machine                                                Devon Thiele • September 2026


The polynomial arithmetic below answers it. Take the check’s three inequalities, add
                                                  √
nothing from quantum mechanics, and the same 2 2 falls out the bottom on its own.
The inequalities themselves come from the NPA matrix, so the physics literature chose
the shape of the check. What no physics does is prove the bound; algebra does that.

A note on the rational approximation in the kernel. The kernel names the ratio-
nal threshold 5657/2000 ≈ 2.8285 for exact rational comparisons (you can verify
                                                               √      √
(5657/2000)2 = 32,001,649/4,000,000 > 8, so 5657/2000 > 8 = 2 2). The real-
                                       √
valued quantum-model bridge uses 8 directly. Those are two different proof sur-
faces, and I keep them apart on purpose: the rational one so a machine can check it in
exact arithmetic, the real one so the quantum bridge has something to bite on.

18.4 What the algebra proves

   Plain reading. Factor the expression. Two terms. One has B0 + B1 , the other has
   B0 − B1 . With B0 , B1 ∈ {±1}, only one can be nonzero at a time, and the nonzero
   one is ±2. Multiply by | A| ≤ 1. The whole expression caps at 2. Average over λ,
   you get |S| ≤ 2. To make it 2, just have both players always answer 1.


Theorem 18.1 (Classical CHSH Bound). For population correlations with a setting-
independent shared-variable distribution, Alice’s local response depending on x, λ and Bob’s
on y, λ, |S| ≤ 2. The constant strategy attains equality. Its four-setting VM witness has zero
charge when every trial uses δ = 0.

Proof. |S| ≤ 2: the XOR-factoring argument from Section 18’s “Why 75% is the
classical ceiling.” Fix any local deterministic strategy and any λ. Alice produces
A x (λ) ∈ {−1, +1}; Bob produces By (λ) ∈ {−1, +1}. The quantity

  A0 (λ) B0 (λ) + A0 (λ) B1 (λ) + A1 (λ) B0 (λ) − A1 (λ) B1 (λ)
                                     = A0 (λ)( B0 (λ) + B1 (λ)) + A1 (λ)( B0 (λ) − B1 (λ))

satisfies | · | ≤ | B0 + B1 | + | B0 − B1 | = 2 (because for B0 , B1 ∈ {±1}, one of | B0 + B1 |
and | B0 − B1 | is 2 and the other is 0, and | A0 |, | A1 | ≤ 1). Average over λ to get |S| ≤ 2.
Mixtures of deterministic strategies are convex combinations, so the same bound holds
by linearity of expectation.

Tightness: the strategy A x ≡ 1, By ≡ 1 gives ⟨ A0 B0 ⟩ = ⟨ A0 B1 ⟩ = ⟨ A1 B0 ⟩ = ⟨ A1 B1 ⟩ =
1, so S = 1 + 1 + 1 − 1 = 2. This is achievable at µ = 0 by a program with all δ = 0
instructions accumulating one trial per setting pair in vm_witness. The Coq witness
is classical_bound_achieved.

   Plain reading. Direct contrapositive. If |S| ≤ 2 for the population-level correlation
   of every LHV, then a population-level S > 2 rules out every LHV. The Bell-test
   contradiction has this form, and it’s a claim about the number an unbounded run

                                             137
The Thiele Machine                                                 Devon Thiele • September 2026


    converges to. The number on any one finite scoreboard is a different thing.


Theorem 18.2 (Non-locality of an exact CHSH violation). An exact correlation E =
( E00 , E01 , E10 , E11 ), with S = E00 + E01 + E10 − E11 and S > 2, cannot arise as the
population-level correlation of any local hidden variable model (the term is built in Section A:
a classical-physics-style theory where outcomes come from shared randomness λ plus local
settings). This is a claim about the idealized correlator, the value an unbounded run of trials
at each setting converges to. It is not a claim about any one finite tally of recorded trials; that
distinction is spelled out below.

Proof. Contrapositive of the Classical CHSH Bound. Any LHV model produces a
locally factorizable correlation by definition: each player’s outcome is a function of
their setting and the shared λ. The theorem above says all such correlations satisfy
|S| ≤ 2. So an idealized correlation with S > 2 cannot have arisen from any LHV
model.


Remark 18.1. A finite scoreboard is not the population correlation.                      A
WitnessCounts tally from a finite run of CHSH_TRIAL instructions is a sample statis-
tic, chsh_stat_from_wc, not the idealized correlator the theorem above bounds. A
local hidden variable model can draw a fresh λ per trial, and a short run can land
away from its own population mean by chance. Run one trial at each of the four set-
tings, with Alice and Bob each answering an independent fair coin flip, an honest LHV
strategy with population correlation zero at every setting: the event “same at (0, 0),
same at (0, 1), same at (1, 0), different at (1, 1)” has probability 1/16 and produces the
finite tally S = 4, the algebraic maximum, with no communication and no quantum
content anywhere in the construction. This is the same finite-sample distinction Zhang,
Glancy, and Knill work out in full for Bell tests (Efficient quantification of experimental
evidence against local realism, arXiv:1303.7464): a population bound of |S| ≤ 2 is not, by
itself, a claim that every finite sample drawn from a local model also satisfies it.

What the kernel proves about a finite tally is narrower, and it does hold: chsh_v
iolation_rules_out_locally_factorizable_coupling shows that a tally
with S > 2 is inconsistent with any single fixed deterministic strategy ( a0 , a1 , b0 , b1 )
replayed identically at every recorded trial (locally_consistent_classical_b
ound is the converse direction: a tally consistent with one fixed deterministic strategy
has |S| ≤ 2). That rules out one specific, narrow explanation of the tally. It does not
rule out a randomized local model that redraws λ trial by trial, which is exactly the
kind of model the coin-flip example above uses to reach S = 4 honestly. Reading a
certified witness violation as physically informative rests on running enough trials to
make the fixed-strategy count and the population count converge, not on an identity
between one finite tally and the exact-correlation theorem above.

   Plain reading. PSD of the fixed matrix and column contractivity are the same


                                               138
The Thiele Machine                                              Devon Thiele • September 2026


    condition. PSD also caps each row, and Cauchy–Schwarz turns those row caps
           √
    into 2 2. That is a statement about this matrix. A quantum behavior in general,
    or a finite local sample, is a different object with its own definition.

Bell’s theorem has another face, and it is worth seeing both. The statistical face is
the one everyone draws: counts going into a number S and the number refusing to


                                             139
The Thiele Machine                                            Devon Thiele • September 2026


stay under 2. But the same wall shows up one floor down, in the language of cou-
plings, where you are no longer talking about averages at all but about whether two
parties can each answer locally and have their answers fit together. A WitnessCount-
consistent locally deterministic strategy here is an honest, almost insultingly simple
thing: four bits, Alice’s answer for each of her two settings and Bob’s for each of his,
fixed in advance with no peeking. Pin that down and you get a separable coupling,
by which I mean one that hands each setting exactly one outcome type, no hedging,
no mixing. The predicate WCLocallyConsistent additionally requires every one
of the four setting buckets to be nonempty, and under that premise the kernel proves
|S| ≤ 2. So read it the other way, which is the way that bites: if a finite tally has S > 2,
there is no single fixed four-bit strategy, repeated identically on every recorded trial,
that explains that tally. It does not rule out a randomized local model that redraws its
hidden variable every trial; the finite-score remark above shows one landing past 2
by luck. The wall is not a fact about statistics, dressed up in structure afterward; it is
already there in the structure, and the statistics are just where you happen to bump
into it first. Going from one finite tally to a claim about the population takes a separate
sampling argument.

What does any of this have to do with computation? Everything, and it comes in
through the witness counters and the certification layer. Picture a trace that sits there
racking up CHSH trial results through CHSH_TRIAL, tally after tally, costing nothing,
because counting is cheap and the universe does not send you a bill for watching it.
Then the trace does the expensive thing: it commits to a claim about them. Checking
the tally is CHSH_LASSERT, which costs at least one whether it passes or traps. Flip-
ping a certification channel is CERTIFY or MORPH_ASSERT, and A2 forces µ ≥ 1 on
that flip, no exceptions. Neither flip checks the claim on its own; the check and the
commitment are separate steps, each priced. So the statistics are free and the claim
about the statistics is not, and that gap is the whole point. You can stare at the data all
day for nothing; the moment you stand up and say you know what it means, some-
where a counter ticks. Throughout, the witness counters carry Alice’s setting x and
Bob’s setting y, the same indexing the formal layer uses, so this is not a side dialect
(Section 11). It is the witness insight taxonomy of Section 6, viewed from the place
where physics hands you a free supply of trials and then charges you for the sentence
you write about them.

18.5 The bit-commitment requirement
If you want cross-correlations above the classical bound to come out attested, not just
observed, the reference kernel makes you pay for a step from the priced class to get
there. nonlocal_correlation_requires_revelation says exactly which: a
trace that starts with the certificate-address channel at zero and ends with it set con-
tains a REVEAL, EMIT, LJOIN, LASSERT, or MORPH_ASSERT. That theorem is about
the channel. It knows nothing about CHSH. Requiring REVEAL in particular for a
super-quantum claim is a runtime policy, and the source says so. The requirement


                                            140
The Thiele Machine                                             Devon Thiele • September 2026


falls straight out of how cost is priced: A2 charges the cert-flip, so the only way to
reach an attested claim is to take the step that flips the cert, and that step costs. The
strictness is downstream of the accounting, not the other way around, and the next
few lines walk through exactly where it gets forced.

So here is where that gets concrete. A single CHSH_TRIAL drops a few setting bits
and outcome bits into the witness counters, and the cross-correlations ⟨ A x By ⟩ are
nothing but those tallies divided through. They are a record of what happened, and
the ledger has no opinion about a record. The accounting only wakes up when you
point at one of those numbers and say it backs a particular claim. That is not a count
any more. It is a stance, and a stance is the one thing the ledger knows how to charge
for, which is why the cost lands precisely on the sentence and never on the arithmetic
underneath it.

And this part is not a hope, it is settled. There is exactly one instruction in the reference
kernel that can push csr_cert_addr off zero, and that is MORPH_ASSERT, and
even then only when the morphism genuinely exists and the property string it carries
is more than an empty NUL byte. So the door to attestation has a single key. Run that
backward and it pins the trace: anything ending in supra_cert had to have walked
through a valid MORPH_ASSERT step on the way, there is no other path that sets
the register, and the kernel will not let you fake one. Which is the same wall from
a closer angle. CHSH_TRIAL counts pile up for free, all the raw data you like, but
the instruction that stands up and commits to the pile is the one that costs, and it is
the only one that even can. What it commits to is a label on an existing morphism.
Whether the label is true of the pile is a separate question, and CHSH_LASSERT is the
priced check that answers part of it.

18.6 Algebraic characterization of the CHSH gate condition
If you only have the patience for one subsection of Section 18, make it this one. Every-
thing before it is the long walk, the honest grind of getting from the machine’s check
to the physics boundary. What lands here is the thing the grind was for: the place
where the check the machine runs and the matrix the physics literature uses turn out
to be the same condition read two ways, for one fixed slice. The rest of the section
earns it. This is what it earns.

Here is what the machine’s check comes to against the NPA boundary. The column-
contractivity conditions are what positive-semidefiniteness of that NPA moment ma-
trix comes to, written out as polynomials. The matrix comes from the physics literature.
The proof that the two conditions agree uses no physics at all; it is pure polynomial
arithmetic. The following biconditional holds.

Positive semidefinite (PSD). A matrix M is positive semidefinite if for every vector v
you can multiply with it, the quantity v T Mv is at least 0. (Here v T is the transpose
of v from Section A: turning a column of numbers on its side; v T Mv is the matrix
product of v T , M, and v, which is just one number.) The intuition: PSD means the

                                            141
The Thiele Machine                                            Devon Thiele • September 2026


matrix never maps a vector
                         ! to something pointing “backwards” relative to the vector.
                     a b
For a 2 × 2 matrix        , PSD reduces to three simple conditions: a ≥ 0, c ≥ 0, and
                    b c
ac − b2 ≥ 0. The first two say the diagonal entries are non-negative. The third says
the determinant (Section A) is non-negative. For larger matrices, the PSD condition
generalises: every principal submatrix (every square subblock you get by picking
some rows-and-columns at the same indices, see Section A) must also be PSD.

Why PSD? In the NPA picture, PSD is the condition for a matrix to be a table of genuine
inner products, which is what correlations from a quantum state are. NPA uses PSD of
a specific 5 × 5 matrix, built from the four CHSH correlators, as its level-1 condition for
quantum realizability. That is the physics reading, and it comes from the NPA papers.
The proofs here don’t supply it. What Coq checks is the matrix: its inequalities and
what follows from them. No Hilbert space, no state, no observables. Two scope words
are load-bearing. For this pinned zero-marginal matrix the PSD test is exactly column
contractivity. The general level-1 NPA test, marginals free, is a looser relaxation, and
the hierarchy tightens it at higher levels (Section 18.7).

18.6.1 The matrix, written out
Here’s the shape of it before the arithmetic. The four correlator numbers go into a
5 × 5 grid. Look at the grid through three windows: two small ones and one bigger
one (Figure 8). Each window has to pass one test, the same test every time, that the
numbers inside it hang together the way a table of real inner products has to. Those
three tests are the three conditions the machine’s integer check runs. Pass all three and
the whole grid passes. Fail one and it doesn’t.

What follows is the formal layer at its most formal: the grid itself, then the determinant
arithmetic that pulls the three conditions out of it, then the proof that they’re enough.
The models wrote that derivation and Coq checked it. If you don’t want the arithmetic,
skip ahead to the theorem it proves, “Slice coherence ⇐⇒ NPA-PSD,” and read on
from there.

Here is the actual 5×5 moment matrix for the zero-marginal CHSH slice. The rows
and columns carry the usual NPA labels: I, A0 , A1 , B0 , B1 . In the physics reading
they are the identity and the two measurements on each side, and the (i, j) entry is the
expected value of Oi O j . In Coq they are just positions in a real matrix. Zero-marginal
means the four one-party slots and the two within-party slots are set to zero, which
leaves the CHSH correlators in the cross positions.

Rows and columns are indexed 0 through 4, in the order { I, A0 , A1 , B0 , B1 }. The

squares to the identity. In Coq the 1s are simply written into the definition. The off-
diagonal entries Exy are the CHSH correlators with Alice’s setting x and Bob’s setting y
(Section A). Everything else is zero because this is the zero-marginal, zero-within-party

                                                            !

                                                                !

E T is the transpose of E from Section A. A standard linear-algebra fact (the Schur
complement criterion, also Section A) says that a block matrix of this shape is positive-
semidefinite if and only if the Schur complement of the upper-left I2 is positive-
semidefinite. The Schur complement here works out to I2 − E T E. The order of the
transpose matters: E T E puts the squared norms (sums of squares) of the columns of E
on its diagonal, while EE T would put the squared norms of the rows. The right one
for this argument is E T E.

Computing E T E entry by entry:

conditions 1 and 2 again) and the determinant must be non-negative, AC − B2 ≥ 0.
That last inequality is condition 3.


                                                 144
The Thiele Machine                                               Devon Thiele • September 2026


That is where the three conditions come from: two from 3×3 submatrices, one from the
Schur complement of the 4×4 block, each one a principal minor forced non-negative by
M being PSD. That derivation runs necessity, PSD of M implies the three conditions.
It does not establish sufficiency; checking these particular minors does not by itself
certify every principal minor of the full 5 × 5; a direct argument for the other direction
follows.
                                                                            !
                                                                   I2 E
The forward direction, directly. Write M in block form as 1 ⊕                 : the (0, 0)
                                                                  E T I2
entry is 1, the rest of row and column 0 are 0 because the marginals vanish, and the
remaining 4 × 4 block is exactly the one just computed. For v = (v0 , x, y) with v0 ∈ R
and x, y ∈ R2 ,

     v T Mv = v20 + x T x + 2x T Ey + y T y = v20 + ∥ x + Ey∥2 + y T ( I2 − E T E)y,

the last step by expanding ∥ x + Ey∥2 = x T x + 2x T Ey + y T E T Ey and cancelling the
y T E T Ey term against its copy in y T ( I2 − E T E)y. The first two terms are non-negative
for every v0 and every x, with no condition on E at all; x is free to range over all of R2 ,
and ∥ x + Ey∥2 ≥ 0 regardless of what value it takes. The entire content is in the third
term: v T Mv ≥ 0 for every choice of v0 , x, y if and only if y T ( I2 − E T E)y ≥ 0 for every
y ∈ R2 , which is exactly PSD of the 2 × 2 matrix I2 − E T E computed above. A 2 × 2
symmetric matrix is PSD iff its diagonal entries are non-negative and its determinant
is non-negative, which is conditions 1, 2, and 3 together. So column-contractivity is
equivalent to PSD of I2 − E T E, which is equivalent to v T Mv ≥ 0 for every v, which is
PSD of M. This is the complete forward direction: three conditions checked once, as
the PSD criterion for one 2 × 2 matrix, not as a claim that a hand-picked list of minors
stands in for all 25 − 1 principal minors of the 5 × 5.

What this fixed completion establishes. The reverse direction (proved as npa_ps
d_implies_column_contractive) is that if M is PSD, the correlators must be
column-contractive. Here is the argument. Take any PSD matrix M and feed it the
specific 5-dimensional vector v = (0, −( E00 y0 + E01 y1 ), −( E10 y0 + E11 y1 ), y0 , y1 ),
where y0 and y1 are free real numbers. The PSD condition v T Mv ≥ 0, after the matrix
algebra, gives a quadratic expression in y0 and y1 : Ay20 − 2By0 y1 + Cy21 ≥ 0 for all real
y0 and y1 . If a quadratic of the form ay2 + 2by + c is non-negative for every real y,
then a ≥ 0 and ac ≥ b2 (the non-negative-discriminant rule from intermediate algebra:
a parabola that never dips below the axis has non-positive discriminant, which after
sign-flipping reads b2 ≤ ac; that one direction is all the argument uses). That closes
condition 3. The diagonal conditions (1 and 2) come from setting y1 = 0 and y0 = 0
separately.

A correlation ( E00 , E01 , E10 , E11 ) is slice-coherent if and only if it is realizable by the
specified zero-marginal, zero-within-party-moment completion. Exactly. Not approxi-
mately. Not “a complete test for physical quantum behavior.” The name describes the


                                              145
The Thiele Machine                                                Devon Thiele • September 2026


test: membership in the fixed algebraic completion used by zero_marginal_npa.
Four ratios can satisfy that test whether their counters came from trials or were written
directly. A passing gate earns the algebraic membership claim. It does not establish
the history of those counters. Nor does it build a quantum state and observables
for the tuple. The biconditional is pure polynomial arithmetic with no project-local
axioms (Print Assumptions on column_contractive_iff_npa_psd reports
only Coq’s standard classical-reals and functional-extensionality stdlib axioms, namely
sig_not_dec, sig_forall_dec, and functional_extensionality_dep, the
same stdlib families Section 18.7 discloses for the Q1+AB theorems; “zero axioms” in
this section means zero project-local, in the sense of Section 23):

   Plain reading. Both directions are pure algebra on the 5 × 5 matrix. Forward: each
   of the three column-contractivity conditions is a principal-submatrix or Schur-
   complement determinant being non-negative, and together they make the whole
   matrix PSD. Reverse: feed the PSD matrix a specific test vector built from (y0 , y1 ).
   What comes out is a quadratic in (y0 , y1 ) that is never negative, so its discriminant
   can’t be positive, which is b2 ≤ ac. That’s condition 3. The diagonal conditions
   fall out at (1, 0) and (0, 1). No physics anywhere; both directions are polynomial-
   arithmetic identities.


Theorem 18.5 (Slice coherence ⇐⇒ NPA-PSD).

                               coherent(E) ⇐⇒ psd-npa(E)

where coherent means the three column-contractivity conditions hold, and psd-npa means
the 5 × 5 specified zero-marginal, zero-within-party-moment matrix is positive semidefinite.

Proof. The full math is in the two derivations of Section 18 preceding this theorem.
Recapitulated for the proof block:

Forward direction (column-contractive ⇒ PSD). Assume the three column-contractivity
conditions hold. The Section 18 derivation showed: condition 1 is exactly the principal-
submatrix determinant on rows/columns {1, 2, 3}; condition 2 is exactly the principal-
submatrix determinant on rows/columns {1, 2, 4}; condition 3 is exactly the Schur-
complement determinant of the 4 × 4 block. Those three minors alone do not settle
PSD; the proof completes squares. At a vector (v0 , x0 , x1 , y0 , y1 ) the quadratic form is
v20 + ( x0 + E00 y0 + E01 y1 )2 + ( x1 + E10 y0 + E11 y1 )2 + yT ( I − ET E) y, and the last term
is the 2 × 2 form with diagonal A, C and off-diagonal − B (notation below), which the
three conditions make non-negative. So the form is non-negative everywhere and the
full 5 × 5 matrix is PSD. The Coq witness is zero_marginal_npa_column_contr
active_implies_psd.

Reverse direction (PSD ⇒ column-contractive). Assume the moment matrix M is PSD.



                                              146
The Thiele Machine                                                Devon Thiele • September 2026


Specialize v T Mv ≥ 0 at the vector
                                                                                     
             v(y0 , y1 ) := 0, −( E00 y0 + E01 y1 ), −( E10 y0 + E11 y1 ), y0 , y1

for free y0 , y1 ∈ R. Computing v T Mv using the explicit moment-matrix entries and
collecting terms gives the quadratic form

                      Ay20 − 2By0 y1 + Cy21 ≥ 0       for all y0 , y1 ∈ R,

where A = 1 − E00     2 − E2 , B = E E + E E , C = 1 − E2 − E2 . Specializing
                            10           00 01     10 11             01    11
at (y0 , y1 ) = (1, 0) and (0, 1) gives A ≥ 0 and C ≥ 0, conditions 1 and 2. The
quadratic-non-negative-discriminant lemma (quadratic_nonneg_discriminan
t), applied at ( a, b, c) = ( A, − B, C ), turns non-negativity for every (y0 , y1 ) into B2 ≤
AC, condition 3. That is one implication; the converse is the forward direction above.
The Coq witness is npa_psd_implies_column_contractive.

The left side is three polynomial inequalities (column contractivity of the specified
zero-marginal, zero-within-party-moment matrix):

                                  2     2
                             1 − E00 − E10 ≥0       (condition 1)

                           10        00 01     10 11           01   11
three conditions of zero_marginal_column_contractive (defined): column
0 norm ≤ 1, column 1 norm ≤ 1, determinant AC − B2 ≥ 0. The right side says
the NPA moment matrix is positive semidefinite. One scope note so this sentence
cannot be quoted into a bigger claim than it makes: for this pinned 5 × 5 matrix, PSD
is exactly column contractivity (that is what the biconditional proves; reading it as
the orthogonal-observables slice, through a Gram decomposition, is standard linear
algebra that is not mechanised here), but level-1 PSD in general, with marginals in
play, is a relaxation, an outer approximation the NPA hierarchy tightens level by level.
Section 18.7 climbs to the level that is exact for CHSH.

The forward direction is zero_marginal_npa_column_contractive_implie
s_psd. The reverse direction: given a PSD NPA matrix, specialize it at the vector
(0, −( E00 y0 + E01 y1 ), −( E10 y0 + E11 y1 ), y0 , y1 ). The PSD condition gives a quadratic-
in-y0 /y1 that is everywhere non-negative. Then quadratic_nonneg_discrimina
nt (∀t, a + 2bt + ct2 ≥ 0 ⇒ b2 ≤ ac) closes the determinant condition. The diagonal
conditions come from y0 = 1, y1 = 0 and y0 = 0, y1 = 1. Both directions are packaged
as the biconditional column_contractive_iff_npa_psd.

What this means for the hierarchy of correlations:



                                              147

   • Classical local hidden variables: |S| ≤ 2.
   • Slice-coherent = realizable by the specified zero-marginal, zero-within-party-
     moment completion (biconditional, both directions, no project-local axioms).
                                                         √
     Every slice-coherent correlation satisfies |S| ≤ 2 2, but NOT every correlation
                 √
     with |S| ≤ 2 2 is slice-coherent: (1, 0, 1, 0) has |S| = 2 but fails condition 1.
   • No-signaling: no constraint beyond | Eab | ≤ 1.
                                                                    2 − E2 =
   • PR box (E00 = E01 = E10 = 1, E11 = −1): condition 1 gives 1 − E00   10
     1 − 1 − 1 = −1 < 0. Fails immediately by direct arithmetic.
   • Tsirelson bound: state_column_contractive_implies_tsirelson
     proves, for the witness-derived correlators of a VM state, slice-coherent ⇒
                            √
     |S|2 ≤ 8 (i.e., |S| ≤ 2 2). The chain factors through PSD: column-contractive
     ⇒ NPA-PSD ⇒ row bounds (npa_psd_zero_marginal_implies_row_bo
     unds) ⇒ Tsirelson. For arbitrary correlators the statement is npa_psd_impli
                                                           √
     es_tsirelson_bound. The contrapositive (|S| > 2 2 ⇒ not slice-coherent)
     follows immediately. The converse is false and not proved.

The gate checks membership in a specified algebraic set. That is the claim it has to earn.
Run the sixteen deterministic local strategies through it: every one of them has all
four correlators at ±1, so condition 1 reads 1 − 1 − 1 = −1 < 0 and CHSH_LASSERT
traps every single one of them, including the all-ones strategy that this section’s own
tightness theorem (classical_bound_achieved) runs at µ = 0 to touch the clas-
sical bound. Those tallies are as true as tallies get, the trace really did accumulate
those counts, and the gate rejects them anyway, because their correlators fail the fixed
orthogonal completion. Marginals alone do not explain this: mixing each determinis-
tic strategy with its simultaneous sign reversal preserves those correlators and gives
zero marginals. In the other direction, a program can run a sequence of CHSH_TRIAL
calls chosen to land the tally inside the slice, rather than earning it by playing the
game honestly against an unpredictable referee, and the gate waves it through the
same as an honest run; LOAD_IMM itself only writes registers, not the vm_witness
buckets CHSH_LASSERT reads, so it cannot forge a tally directly, but a program is free
to choose its own CHSH_TRIAL inputs and drive the buckets to any reachable value it
likes. Whether a tally was earned by play against a genuine referee or walked there
on purpose is the kind of fact the receipt discipline of Section 15 meters, not this op-
code. So the correct reading of the boundary is set-theoretic, not moral. The classical
polytope and the slice-coherent set intersect, uniform noise (0, 0, 0, 0) sits comfortably
in both, but neither contains the other: (1, 0, 1, 0), reachable by a classical mixture and
carrying a legal |S| = 2, fails condition 1 (1 − 12 − 12 = −1 < 0), while every point
satisfying the fixed orthogonal completion passes. What a successful CHSH_LASSERT
certifies is that the counters cohere with the zero-marginal slice, no more and no less,
and the Tsirelson corollary is inherited from that membership, not from any power to
sniff out forged data.



                                           148
The Thiele Machine                                                Devon Thiele • September 2026


Kernel-level enforcement (the CHSH_LASSERT opcode). Here is the thing about a
certification bit: by itself it is just a bit. A generic CERTIFY can set vm_certified
:= true and never once glance at the witness counters that are supposed to earn
it. That is a rubber stamp with the ink already on it, and a rubber stamp is exactly
what the whole discipline is built to forbid. So the certification channel cannot lean
on CERTIFY for this discipline; it needs an opcode that does the looking, that reads
the evidence before it is allowed to say the word. That opcode is CHSH_LASSERT,
and it refuses to advance unless the counts are slice-coherent, column-contractive. It
does not itself set vm_certified or csr_cert_addr. It reads the WitnessCounts
buckets, the counts of how the two parties actually came out together and apart, and
it computes the three integer-arithmetic conditions column_contractive_check_
witness in Z-arithmetic, no real and no rational ever evaluated at runtime, because
the moment you let floating point near a soundness check you have smuggled in a
place for the answer to drift. If the counts satisfy the three conditions, the program
counter advances and the run goes on. If they do not, there is no negotiating: the
step traps to LASSERT_TRAP_PC and latches vm_err, and the incoherent run goes no
further. Two theorems close the chain to NPA-PSD; both are stated and proved below.

   Plain reading. Integer arithmetic upstairs, real arithmetic downstairs, IZR is the
   elevator. The check throws out an empty setting bucket before it does anything
   else: N00 , N01 , N10 , N11 > 0 are part of the check itself. A witness record with two
   settings never exercised, all trials piled onto the other two, fails on those positivity
   checks and never reaches the polynomial conditions. With positivity in hand,
   write the three column-contractivity conditions in terms of the counts, cleared of
   denominators, and you have polynomial inequalities in Z. Cast to R via IZR; that
   keeps ≥ 0. Divide back through by the strictly positive trial counts and you get
   the real-valued conditions. The whole route avoids floating point at every step:
   integers in, reals out, division at the boundary, and the boundary is never zero.


Theorem 18.6 (Integer-check soundness, column_contractive_check_witne
ss_sound). Let w be the WitnessCount record with eight non-negative-integer fields
wc_samexy , wc_diffxy for ( x, y) ∈ {0, 1}2 . Define Nxy = wc_samexy + wc_diffxy .
The integer-only check column_contractive_check_witness is the conjunction of
two parts: the four strict-positivity conjuncts N00 > 0, N01 > 0, N10 > 0, N11 > 0, and
three Z-arithmetic inequalities on the counts, all written with no division. If the integer check
returns true, then every Nxy is a positive integer, so

                                     wc_samexy − wc_diffxy
                             Exy =
                                              Nxy

is an ordinary quotient with a positive denominator, and E = ( E00 , E01 , E10 , E11 ) ∈ [−1, 1]4
satisfies the three column-contractivity conditions of Section 18. This is a different fact from
the general zero-trial convention of Section A, which sets Exy = 0 when Nxy = 0 so that the


                                              149
The Thiele Machine                                              Devon Thiele • September 2026


CHSH score S is defined on every witness record. That convention lets S be computed at all; it
plays no role here, because a passing check already guarantees no setting pair is empty.

Proof. The four positivity conjuncts are checked first, independently of the three poly-
nomial conditions: the boolean function returns false the moment any Nxy = 0,
before the polynomial terms are even compared. A true result therefore rules out ev-
ery empty setting pair by construction. Take the record wc_same00 = wc_same01 = 1
with the other six counters zero: N10 = N11 = 0, so the positivity conjuncts for settings
(1, 0) and (1, 1) fail and the check returns false outright, without ever reaching the
polynomial comparison. No witness record with an empty setting pair passes.

For the remaining three conditions, multiplying through by Nxy
gives polynomial inequalities in the non-negative integer counts:
